1.  **Initialize:**
    *   Queue: `[(B, [B])]` (node, path)
    *   Visited: `{B}`

2.  **Level 0 (Length 0):**
    *   Dequeue `(B, [B])`.
    *   Neighbors of B: H, C, N.
    *   Enqueue `(H, [B, H])`, `(C, [B, C])`, `(N, [B, N])`.
    *   Visited: `{B, H, C, N}`.

3.  **Level 1 (Length 1):**
    *   Dequeue `(H, [B, H])`.
        *   Neighbors of H: G, E, I (B is already visited).
        *   Enqueue `(G, [B, H, G])`, `(E, [B, H, E])`, `(I, [B, H, I])`.
        *   Visited: `{B, H, C, N, G, E, I}`.
    *   Dequeue `(C, [B, C])`.
        *   Neighbors of C: O (B, N, I are already visited).
        *   Enqueue `(O, [B, C, O])`.
        *   Visited: `{B, H, C, N, G, E, I, O}`.
    *   Dequeue `(N, [B, N])`.
        *   Neighbors of N: Y (B, C, O are already visited).
        *   Enqueue `(Y, [B, N, Y])`.
        *   Visited: `{B, H, C, N, G, E, I, O, Y}`.

4.  **Level 2 (Length 2):**
    *   Dequeue `(G, [B, H, G])`.
        *   Neighbors of G: K, F, X (H is already visited).
        *   Enqueue `(K, [B, H, G, K])`, `(F, [B, H, G, F])`, `(X, [B, H, G, X])`.
        *   Visited: `{B, H, C, N, G, E, I, O, Y, K, F, X}`.
    *   Dequeue `(E, [B, H, E])`.
        *   Neighbors of E: L, M, F (H is already visited).
        *   Node M is the target node!
        *   The path found is `[B, H, E, M]`.
        *   The length of this path is 3.

Since BFS guarantees that the first time the target node is reached, the path found is the shortest, we can conclude that the shortest path has a length of 3.

**Verification of the path [B, H, E, M]:**
*   Is B directly connected to H? Yes.
*   Is H directly connected to E? Yes.
*   Is E directly connected to M? Yes.

All connections are valid.

Final Answer: Length: 3, Path: [B, H, E, M]

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 5
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are M and B.
2.  **Identify Initial Branches from M:** From node M, there are four direct connections to white nodes: L, E, F, and J. These represent the main branches for paths originating from M.
3.  **Analyze Paths through L, E, F (Left Side):**
    *   Paths starting M -> L: M-L-E-B, M-L-E-G-B.
    *   Paths starting M -> E: M-E-B, M-E-G-B.
    *   Paths starting M -> F: M-F-G-B, M-F-X-G-B, M-F-K-G-B.
    All these paths from M (via L, E, or F) eventually converge and pass through either node E or node G to reach B.
    *   If we remove E, paths M-L-E-B, M-L-E-G-B, M-E-B, M-E-G-B are all severed. The remaining paths from this side are M-F-G-B, M-F-X-G-B, M-F-K-G-B. All these remaining paths pass through G.
    *   Therefore, to disconnect M from B via the L, E, F branch, we must remove both E and G. This forms a cut set of size 2 for this branch: {E, G}.
    *   Alternatively, if we remove E, the paths M-F-G-B, M-F-X-G-B, M-F-K-G-B remain. If we then remove F, these paths are also cut. So {E, F} is another cut set of size 2 for this branch.

4.  **Analyze Paths through J (Right Side):**
    *   Paths starting M -> J: M-J-P-O-C-B, M-J-P-O-N-B, M-J-P-I-C-B, M-J-P-I-H-G-B, M-J-P-I-H-B.
    All these paths from M (via J) must pass through node P. Node D is a leaf node connected to J but not to B.
    *   Therefore, removing node P will disconnect all paths from M to B that go through J. This forms a cut set of size 1 for this branch: {P}.

5.  **Combine Cut Sets:** To disconnect M from B entirely, we need to sever both the left-side paths and the right-side paths.
    *   Using {E, G} for the left side and {P} for the right side, we get a combined cut set: [E, G, P]. This set has 3 nodes.
    *   Using {E, F} for the left side and {P} for the right side, we get a combined cut set: [E, F, P]. This set also has 3 nodes.

6.  **Verify Minimum Number of Nodes (Menger's Theorem):** To confirm that 3 is the minimum, we look for node-disjoint paths between M and B (excluding M and B themselves).
    *   Path 1: M - E - B (Intermediate node: E)
    *   Path 2: M - F - G - B (Intermediate nodes: F, G)
    *   Path 3: M - J - P - I - H - B (Intermediate nodes: J, P, I, H)
    The sets of intermediate nodes {E}, {F, G}, and {J, P, I, H} are mutually disjoint. Since there are 3 node-disjoint paths, by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect M and B.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since we found a cut set of size 3 ([E, G, P] or [E, F, P]) and determined that at least 3 nodes are required, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [E, G, P]

Image name: Tu_task3_gt2_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 5
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F and J. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect F from J.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node F (black) is connected to: G, K, X, P.
    *   Node J (black) is connected to: P, M, D.

2.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   To isolate J, we would need to remove all its direct connections: (J, P), (J, M), and (J, D). This would be a cut of size 3.
    *   To isolate F, we would need to remove all its direct connections: (F, G), (F, K), (F, X), and (F, P). This would be a cut of size 4.
    *   Since we are looking for the *minimum* number of edges, a cut of size 3 is a candidate, but we should check for smaller cuts.

3.  **Explore paths and potential bottlenecks:**
    Let's identify all possible paths from F to J.
    *   **Direct path through P:** F - P - J
    *   **Paths through P and D:** F - P - D - J
    *   **Paths through P and M:**
        *   F - P - O - C - B - E - M - J
        *   F - P - O - N - C - B - E - M - J
        *   F - P - O - N - B - E - M - J
    *   **Paths through G/K/X and then P:**
        *   F - G - H - I - P - J
        *   F - G - H - I - P - D - J
        *   F - G - H - I - P - O - C - B - E - M - J
        *   (Similar paths starting F-K or F-X, which merge into G-H-I-P...)

4.  **Identify a potential cut set of size 2:**
    Let's consider the subgraph formed by F and its immediate neighbors that are not J: {F, G, K, X}.
    *   F is connected to G, K, X.
    *   G is connected to F, K, X, H.
    *   K is connected to F, G, X.
    *   X is connected to F, G, K.
    This set of nodes {F, G, K, X} forms a connected component.

    Now, let's identify all edges that connect this component {F, G, K, X} to the rest of the graph (which includes J).
    *   The edge (F, P) connects F to P.
    *   The edge (G, H) connects G to H.

    All other edges within this component (e.g., (F, G), (F, K), (F, X), (G, K), (G, X), (K, X)) do not lead outside this component.

    Therefore, any path from F to J *must* pass through either the edge (F, P) or the edge (G, H) to leave the {F, G, K, X} component and reach J.

5.  **Test the proposed cut set:**
    If we remove the edges (F, P) and (G, H):
    *   The edge (F, P) is removed, breaking all direct paths from F to P.
    *   The edge (G, H) is removed, breaking all paths from G (and thus from F, K, X via G) to H. Since H is the only node outside {F, G, K, X} that G connects to, this effectively isolates the {F, G, K, X} component from the rest of the graph via G.

    After removing these two edges, the nodes {F, G, K, X} become completely disconnected from the rest of the graph. Since J is not part of this {F, G, K, X} component, F is now completely disconnected from J.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    The cut set [(F, P), (G, H)] successfully disconnects F from J. This cut set has 2 edges. Since a cut of size 1 is not possible (as there are clearly multiple independent paths), 2 is the minimum number of edges.

The final answer is based on the reasoning above.

Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(F, P), (G, H)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['F', 'E'], ['G', 'H']]}

Image name: POGBa_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 5
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H (black) and N (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect H and N.

1.  **Identify the neighbors of the target nodes:**
    *   Neighbors of H: G, I, B
    *   Neighbors of N: C, O, B

2.  **Consider simple cut sets:**
    *   To disconnect N from the rest of the graph, we can remove all edges connected to N. These are (N,B), (N,C), and (N,O). This would be a cut set of size 3.
    *   Similarly, to disconnect H from the rest of the graph, we can remove all edges connected to H. These are (H,G), (H,I), and (H,B). This would also be a cut set of size 3.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut (e.g., size 1 or 2) is possible:**
    According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes (the edge-connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes. If we can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size must be at least 3.

    Let's try to find 3 edge-disjoint paths from H to N:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        Edges: {(H,B), (B,N)}
    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
        Edges: {(H,I), (I,C), (C,N)}
    *   **Path 3:** H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O - N
        Edges: {(H,G), (G,F), (F,E), (E,M), (M,J), (J,P), (P,O), (O,N)}

    Let's verify that these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint (i.e., they do not share any common edges):
    *   Edges in Path 1: (H,B), (B,N)
    *   Edges in Path 2: (H,I), (I,C), (C,N)
    *   Edges in Path 3: (H,G), (G,F), (F,E), (E,M), (M,J), (J,P), (P,O), (O,N)

    Upon inspection, all edges listed for Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are unique and do not overlap. Therefore, these three paths are edge-disjoint.

4.  **Conclusion on minimum cut size:**
    Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.
    We have already identified a cut set of size 3: [(N,B), (N,C), (N,O)]. Removing these three edges completely isolates node N, thus ensuring no path exists between H and N.

5.  **Final verification:**
    *   Removing (N,B), (N,C), and (N,O) makes N an isolated node.
    *   No path from H (or any other node) can reach N.
    *   The number of edges removed is 3.
    *   This is the minimum possible, as confirmed by the existence of 3 edge-disjoint paths.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(N, B), (N, C), (N, O)]

Image name: PGS_task3_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 5
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and H (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from H.

1.  **Identify the direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node E is connected to: L, M, F, B.
    *   Node H is connected to: G, B, I.

2.  **Consider the edges directly connected to H:**
    Any path from E to H must eventually use one of the edges connected to H: (B, H), (G, H), or (I, H). If we remove all three of these edges, H becomes isolated, and thus no path can exist from E to H. This gives us a potential cut set of size 3: `[(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]`.

3.  **Check if a smaller cut set (e.g., size 1 or 2) is possible:**
    *   **Size 1:** It's clear that removing a single edge will not disconnect E and H, as there are multiple distinct routes between them. For example, removing (B, H) would still leave paths through G and I.
    *   **Size 2:** Let's test combinations of two edges, especially those connected to H, as they are critical.
        *   If we remove `(B, H)` and `(G, H)`: A path still exists via I, e.g., E - B - C - I - H. (Note: E-B-C-I-H is a valid path, as B is connected to C, C to I, and I to H).
        *   If we remove `(B, H)` and `(I, H)`: A path still exists via G, e.g., E - F - G - H.
        *   If we remove `(G, H)` and `(I, H)`: A path still exists via B, e.g., E - B - H.
    Since removing any two edges directly connected to H does not disconnect E from H, the minimum cut must be at least 3.

4.  **Confirm the minimum using Menger's Theorem (finding edge-disjoint paths):**
    Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths from E to H:
    *   **Path 1 (P1):** E - B - H
        Edges: `(E, B)`, `(B, H)`
    *   **Path 2 (P2):** E - F - G - H
        Edges: `(E, F)`, `(F, G)`, `(G, H)`
    *   **Path 3 (P3):** E - L - M - F - K - J - I - H
        Edges: `(E, L)`, `(L, M)`, `(M, F)`, `(F, K)`, `(K, J)`, `(J, I)`, `(I, H)`

    Let's verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint (i.e., they do not share any common edges):
    *   P1 uses `(E, B)` and `(B, H)`.
    *   P2 uses `(E, F)`, `(F, G)`, and `(G, H)`.
    *   P3 uses `(E, L)`, `(L, M)`, `(M, F)`, `(F, K)`, `(K, J)`, `(J, I)`, and `(I, H)`.

    Upon inspection, these three paths indeed do not share any common edges. Therefore, there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H. This confirms that the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we found a cut set of size 3 (by removing all edges directly connected to H) and we confirmed that the minimum cut must be at least 3 (by finding 3 edge-disjoint paths), the minimum number of edges to remove is 3. The cut set `[(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]` achieves this by isolating node H.

The final answer is structured as requested.

Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

Image name: PGS_task2_gt3_PAIR1
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 5
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and J. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and J, without removing E or J themselves.

First, I will identify all direct neighbors of E: L, M, F, B.
Any path from E to J must start by traversing one of these four nodes.

Let's analyze the paths based on the first node from E:

1.  **Paths starting with E -> L:**
    The only path from L towards J is L -> M -> F. So, any path starting E -> L must pass through M and then F.
    Example: E -> L -> M -> F -> K -> J

2.  **Paths starting with E -> M:**
    The paths from M towards J are M -> F. So, any path starting E -> M must pass through F.
    Example: E -> M -> F -> K -> J

3.  **Paths starting with E -> F:**
    Any path starting E -> F directly passes through F.
    Example: E -> F -> K -> J

4.  **Paths starting with E -> B:**
    Any path starting E -> B directly passes through B.
    Example: E -> B -> H -> I -> J

From this analysis, it is clear that any path from E to J must pass through either node F (for paths starting E-L, E-M, or E-F) or node B (for paths starting E-B).

Therefore, if I remove both node F and node B, all possible paths from E to J will be severed. This constitutes a cut set of size 2.

To confirm this is the *minimum* number of nodes, I must verify that no single node removal can disconnect E and J.
Let's test removing each potential single node:
*   If I remove F: Paths like E -> B -> H -> I -> J would still exist.
*   If I remove B: Paths like E -> F -> K -> J would still exist.
*   If I remove any other single node (e.g., K, I, P, H, G, C, N, O, D, L, M, X, Y), there would always be at least one path remaining between E and J. For example, if I remove K, paths like E -> B -> H -> I -> J still exist. If I remove I, paths like E -> F -> K -> J still exist.

Since removing any single node does not disconnect E and J, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.
As we found a cut set of size 2 ([F, B]), this is indeed the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [F, B]

Image name: FD-FR_task3_gt2_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 5
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G (black) and N (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from N.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node G is connected to: X, K, F, H.
    *   Node N is connected to: B, C, O, Y.

2.  **Analyze the overall structure and potential "bottlenecks":**
    *   All paths originating from G must pass through one of its neighbors (X, K, F, H).
    *   All paths reaching N must pass through one of its neighbors (B, C, O, Y).
    *   The graph can be conceptually divided into a "G-side" and an "N-side". The "N-side" can be considered the cluster of nodes directly connected to N, which are {N, B, C, O, Y}.

3.  **Consider a simple cut: removing all edges directly connected to N.**
    *   Edges: (N, B), (N, C), (N, O), (N, Y).
    *   If these 4 edges are removed, N becomes an isolated node, thus completely disconnected from G.
    *   This is a valid cut set of size 4. This establishes an upper bound for the minimum number of edges to remove.

4.  **Explore if a smaller cut (e.g., 3 edges) is possible.**
    *   Let's consider the nodes B, C, O. If these three nodes were removed (a vertex cut), N would be disconnected from the rest of the graph (since Y is only connected to N and O, and O would be removed). This is a vertex cut of size 3. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum edge cut must be at least the minimum vertex cut. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

5.  **Attempt to find an edge cut of size 3:**
    *   **Strategy 1: Removing 3 edges directly connected to N.**
        *   If we remove any 3 edges from {(N, B), (N, C), (N, O), (N, Y)}, one edge will remain. For example, if we remove (N, B), (N, C), (N, O), the edge (N, Y) remains. A path like G -> ... -> O -> Y -> N would still exist (as O is connected to Y, and Y to N). This applies to any combination of 3 edges directly connected to N. Thus, removing 3 edges directly connected to N is not sufficient.

    *   **Strategy 2: Identifying "gateway" edges to the N-cluster.**
        Let's define the "N-cluster" as the set of nodes {N, B, C, O, Y}. We need to find edges that connect the rest of the graph (the "G-side") to this N-cluster.
        The edges that connect nodes outside {N, B, C, O, Y} to nodes within {B, C, O, Y} are:
        *   (H, B) - connects H (G-side) to B (N-cluster)
        *   (E, B) - connects E (G-side) to B (N-cluster)
        *   (I, C) - connects I (G-side) to C (N-cluster)
        *   (P, O) - connects P (G-side) to O (N-cluster)
        This set of edges, [(H, B), (E, B), (I, C), (P, O)], forms a cut set of size 4. If these 4 edges are removed, no path can reach B, C, or O from the G-side. Since Y is only connected to N and O, and O cannot be reached, Y also cannot be reached from the G-side. Thus, N is disconnected.

    *   **Attempt to find a cut of 3 edges from this set of 4:**
        Let's test if removing any 3 of these 4 edges leaves a path:
        *   If we remove [(H, B), (E, B), (I, C)]: A path via (P, O) still exists. For example, G-H-I-P-O-N. (H, I) is not cut, (I, P) is not cut, (P, O) is not cut, (O, N) is not cut.
        *   If we remove [(H, B), (E, B), (P, O)]: A path via (I, C) still exists. For example, G-H-I-C-N. (H, I) is not cut, (I, C) is not cut, (C, N) is not cut.
        *   If we remove [(H, B), (I, C), (P, O)]: A path via (E, B) still exists. For example, G-F-E-B-N. (F, E) is not cut, (E, B) is not cut, (B, N) is not cut.
        *   If we remove [(E, B), (I, C), (P, O)]: A path via (H, B) still exists. For example, G-H-B-N. (G, H) is not cut, (H, B) is not cut, (B, N) is not cut.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we have established that the minimum cut is at least 3, and we have shown that no combination of 3 edges (either directly connected to N or from the "gateway" edges) can disconnect G from N, the minimum number of edges must be 4. We have found two valid cut sets of size 4:
    *   [(N, B), (N, C), (N, O), (N, Y)]
    *   [(H, B), (E, B), (I, C), (P, O)]

The second set is chosen as it represents a cut further from the target node N, severing the main pathways into the N-cluster.

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(H, B), (E, B), (I, C), (P, O)]

    *   **Paths starting E -> F:**
        *   E - F - G - H

        *   E - L - M - F - X - G - H
        *   E - L - M - F - K - G - H
        These paths also rely on node L, then M, then F, and then G to reach H.

    From the path analysis, it's clear that all paths from E to H fall into two main categories:
    *   Paths that go through node **B**.
    *   Paths that go through node **F** (and then necessarily through node **G** to reach H, as G is the only node connecting F's cluster to H).

    Therefore, to disconnect E and H, we must remove at least one node from the "B-pathway" and at least one node from the "F-G-pathway".

6.  **Determine the minimum cut set:**
    *   To break all paths in the "B-pathway", we must remove node **B**.
    *   To break all paths in the "F-G-pathway", we can remove either node **F** or node **G**.

    This leads to two possible minimal cut sets of size 2:
    *   **[B, F]**: Removing B severs all paths through B. Removing F severs all paths through F (and M, L which funnel through F).
    *   **[B, G]**: Removing B severs all paths through B. Removing G severs all paths through G (which are the only way from F's cluster to H).

    A single node removal is not sufficient:
    *   Removing B leaves paths like E-F-G-H.
    *   Removing F leaves paths like E-B-H.
    *   Removing G leaves paths like E-B-H.
    *   Removing I leaves paths like E-B-H and E-F-G-H.

    Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [B, F]

    *   Node N is connected to: B, C, Y.

    *   To isolate H, I can remove all edges connected to it: (H, B), (H, G), (H, I). This is a cut set of size 3.
    *   To isolate N, I can remove all edges connected to it: (N, B), (N, C), (N, Y). This is a cut set of size 3.
    Since I have found a cut set of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 3.

3.  **Attempt to find a smaller cut (size 1 or 2):**
    *   **Cut of size 1:** Removing any single edge will not disconnect H and N. For example, if (H,B) is removed, paths like H-G-B-N or H-I-C-N still exist. If (N,B) is removed, paths like H-B-C-N or H-I-C-N still exist. Thus, a cut of size 1 is not possible.
    *   **Cut of size 2:** Let's try to find two edges whose removal disconnects H and N. This is equivalent to checking if there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N (by Menger's Theorem). If there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut is 3.

4.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths between H and N:**
    I will try to find three paths from H to N that do not share any common edges.

    *   **Path 1 (P1): H - B - N**
        Edges: {(H, B), (B, N)}

    *   **Path 2 (P2): H - G - E - B - C - N**
        Edges: {(H, G), (G, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, N)}
        Let's check for disjointness with P1:
        - (H,G) is not in P1.
        - (G,E) is not in P1.
        - (E,B) is not in P1.
        - (B,C) is not in P1.
        - (C,N) is not in P1.
        P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint.

    *   **Path 3 (P3): H - I - C - Y - N**
        Edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, Y), (Y, N)}
        Let's check for disjointness with P1 and P2:
        - (H,I) is not in P1 or P2.
        - (I,C) is not in P1 or P2.
        - (C,Y) is not in P1 or P2.
        - (Y,N) is not in P1 or P2.
        P1, P2, and P3 are all edge-disjoint.

5.  **Conclusion based on Menger's Theorem:**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N, Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges required to disconnect H and N is at least 3.
    As I previously identified a cut set of size 3 (e.g., removing all edges connected to H or all edges connected to N), this confirms that the minimum number of edges to remove is exactly 3.

6.  **Select a minimal cut set:**
    One such minimal cut set is to remove all edges directly connected to H.

Image name: PGS_task3_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 5
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and I. The task is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect G from I. This is a minimum cut problem, which by Menger's Theorem (edge version) is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between G and I.

First, I will identify edge-disjoint paths from G to I. Edge-disjoint means that no two paths share any common edge.

1.  **Path 1: G - H - I**
    Edges used: (G, H), (H, I)

2.  **Path 2: G - K - J - I**
    Edges used: (G, K), (K, J), (J, I)
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as it uses different nodes and edges.

3.  **Path 3: G - F - M - L - E - B - N - C - I**
    Edges used: (G, F), (F, M), (M, L), (L, E), (E, B), (B, N), (N, C), (C, I)
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2, as it uses a completely different set of edges.

Now, I need to check if there is a fourth edge-disjoint path.
From G, the remaining unvisited neighbor (not used in P1, P2, P3) is X.
*   If a path starts with G - X:
    *   X is connected to F and K.
    *   (X, F) would lead to F, which is used by Path 3.
    *   (X, K) would lead to K, which is used by Path 2.
    Therefore, any path starting with G-X would eventually use an edge already part of Path 2 or Path 3, meaning it cannot be edge-disjoint from them.

Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect G and I is at least 3.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects G from I.
A simple way to disconnect a node from the rest of the graph is to remove all its outgoing edges (or incoming, depending on perspective).
Consider the edges directly connected to G: (G, F), (G, X), (G, K), (G, H).
If I remove (G, F), (G, K), and (G, H):
*   G can no longer reach F.
*   G can no longer reach K.
*   G can no longer reach H.
*   G is still connected to X via (G, X). However, X is only connected to F and K. Since (G, F) and (G, K) are removed, G cannot reach F or K via X either.
Therefore, removing the edges (G, F), (G, K), and (G, H) completely isolates G from the rest of the graph, and thus from I.

This cut set has 3 edges. Since we determined that at least 3 edges are required, this set is a minimal cut set.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, F), (G, K), (G, H)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['F', 'E'], ['G', 'H']]}

            *   Path 4: (F, K), (K, J), (J, P)

I have identified 4 edge-disjoint paths between G and I. This means that at least 4 edges must be removed to disconnect G and I.

2.  Removing (I, C) breaks Path 2 (G-B-C-I) and any other path ending at C-I.

4.  Removing (I, P) breaks Path 4 (G-F-K-J-P-I) and any other path ending at P-I.

Since all direct connections to I are severed, no path from G (or any other node) to I can exist. This cut set has a size of 4.

The target nodes are F (top right, black) and J (bottom left, black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between F and J.

First, I identify the direct connections from F and J to the rest of the graph:
*   Node F is connected to E, X, and K.
*   Node J is connected to M, P, and D.

A simple upper bound for the number of edges to remove is to isolate one of the target nodes. For example, removing all edges connected to F: (F, E), (F, X), (F, K) would disconnect F from the graph, thus disconnecting it from J. This would be 3 edges. Similarly, removing all edges connected to J: (J, M), (J, P), (J, D) would also be 3 edges.

Now, I will try to find a smaller cut set, specifically looking for a cut of 2 edges.
To disconnect F and J, all paths between them must be broken. I observe the overall structure of the graph:
1.  All paths originating from F must pass through either node E or node G (since F connects to E, X, K, and X and K both connect to G).
2.  All paths terminating at J must pass through nodes M, P, or D.

Let's consider the "main channels" through which paths flow from the F-side to the J-side:
*   **Channel 1 (via E):** Paths starting F-E-...
*   **Channel 2 (via G):** Paths starting F-X-G-... or F-K-G-...

Let's try to find two edges that could cut all these paths.

**Attempt 1: Cut (E, B) and (G, H)**
*   If I remove (E, B), all paths from F-E that go through B are cut. This includes paths to P and D that originate from E and pass through B (e.g., F-E-B-N-Y-O-P-J, F-E-B-H-I-D-J).
*   If I remove (G, H), all paths from F-X-G or F-K-G that go through H are cut. This includes all paths from the G-branch, as G is only connected to X, K, and H.
*   However, paths like F-E-M-J and F-E-L-M-J are *not* cut by (E, B) or (G, H). These paths still exist.
    *   Therefore, [(E, B), (G, H)] is not a valid cut set.

This indicates that a cut of 2 edges is likely not possible, as we need to address the paths through M separately. To cut all paths to M, we would need to remove both (E, M) and (L, M), which already uses 2 edges, leaving no edges to cut paths to P and D. This suggests the minimum cut is at least 3.

Let's try to find a cut set of 3 edges that is not simply isolating F or J.
Consider the following set of edges: **[(E, B), (G, H), (M, J)]**

Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects F and J:

1.  **Consider paths starting from F and going through E:**
    *   Any path from F-E that goes towards M (e.g., F-E-M-J or F-E-L-M-J) will be cut by the removal of **(M, J)**. (Note: M is only connected to E, L, and J. So (M,J) cuts all paths to J via M).
    *   Any path from F-E that goes towards B (e.g., F-E-B-N-Y-O-P-J, F-E-B-H-I-D-J) will be cut by the removal of **(E, B)**.
    *   Since all paths from E either go to M or B (E is connected to F, M, L, B), and we have cut both these branches, all paths from F through E are now broken.

2.  **Consider paths starting from F and going through X or K (i.e., through G):**
    *   Nodes X and K are connected to G. Therefore, any path from F through X or K must eventually pass through G.
    *   Node G is connected to X, K, and H. So, any path from G to the rest of the graph (towards J) must pass through H.
    *   The removal of **(G, H)** will cut all paths originating from F that pass through G.

Since all paths from F must pass through either E or G, and we have successfully cut all paths through E and all paths through G, F and J are completely disconnected.

The number of edges removed is 3. Since we could not find a valid cut set of 2 edges, and we found a valid cut set of 3 edges, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, B), (G, H), (M, J)]

Image name: FD-K_task3_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 5
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, E and H.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are E and H.

2.  **Identify direct connections and immediate neighbors:**
    *   Node E is connected to: H, F, G, L, M.
    *   Node H is connected to: E, F, G, B, I.

3.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Edge Version):** The minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths from E to H.

    *   **Path 1:** E - H
        *   Edges: (E,H)

    *   **Path 2:** E - F - H
        *   Edges: (E,F), (F,H)
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

    *   **Path 3:** E - G - H
        *   Edges: (E,G), (G,H)
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2 (as (E,G) is distinct from (E,F), and (G,H) is distinct from (F,H)).

    *   **Path 4:** E - M - J - I - H
        *   Edges: (E,M), (M,J), (J,I), (I,H)
        *   Let's check for edge-disjointness with previous paths:
            *   (E,M) is distinct from (E,H), (E,F), (E,G).
            *   (M,J) is distinct from all edges in Paths 1, 2, 3.
            *   (J,I) is distinct from all edges in Paths 1, 2, 3.
            *   (I,H) is distinct from (E,H), (F,H), (G,H).
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Paths 1, 2, 3.

    *   **Attempt to find Path 5:** We need a path that does not use any of the edges from Paths 1, 2, 3, 4.
        *   Edges used so far: (E,H), (E,F), (F,H), (E,G), (G,H), (E,M), (M,J), (J,I), (I,H).
        *   Remaining connections from E: (E,L).
        *   Remaining connections to H: (H,B).
        *   Let's try to find a path from E-L to H-B: E - L - M - J - P - C - B - H.
        *   This path uses the edge (M,J), which is already used in Path 4.
        *   Therefore, this path is NOT edge-disjoint from Path 4.
        *   Upon closer inspection of the graph, all paths from E (via L or M) to H (via B or I) must pass through the edge (M,J). Node M is connected to E, L, J. Node L is connected to E, M. So any path from E through L or M to the right side of the graph must use (M,J) to reach J, and from J to I or P, and then to H.
        *   This confirms that there are no more than 4 edge-disjoint paths.

4.  **Conclusion on minimum number of edges:** Since there are 4 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges that must be removed is 4.

5.  **Find a cut set of 4 edges:** We need to find a set of 4 edges whose removal disconnects E and H. Based on the analysis of paths and bottlenecks:
    *   The direct path (E,H) must be cut. (1 edge)
    *   Paths through the F/G cluster: E-F-H, E-G-H, etc. These can be cut by removing (F,H) and (G,H). (2 edges)
    *   Paths through the L/M/J/P/I/C/B cluster: E-M-J-I-H, E-L-M-J-P-C-B-H, etc. As identified, (M,J) is a bottleneck for all paths from E (via L or M) to H (via B or I). Removing (M,J) will cut all these paths. (1 edge)

    Let's propose the cut set: **[(E,H), (F,H), (G,H), (M,J)]**

6.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   **Edge (E,H):** Directly cuts the path E-H.
    *   **Edge (F,H):** Cuts all paths from E to H that pass through node F and then directly to H (e.g., E-F-H, E-G-F-H, E-K-F-H, E-X-F-H).
    *   **Edge (G,H):** Cuts all paths from E to H that pass through node G and then directly to H (e.g., E-G-H, E-F-G-H, E-K-G-H, E-X-G-H).
    *   **Edge (M,J):** Cuts all paths from E to H that pass through nodes L or M and then proceed through J to reach H.
        *   Paths like E-M-J-I-H, E-M-J-P-I-H, E-M-J-P-C-B-H are cut.
        *   Paths like E-L-M-J-I-H, E-L-M-J-P-I-H, E-L-M-J-P-C-B-H are cut.
        *   Since M is only connected to E, L, J, and L is only connected to E, M, any path from E through L or M to H must eventually pass through J (via M-J). From J, paths lead to I or P, which can then lead to H (I-H, P-I-H, P-C-B-H). Therefore, removing (M,J) effectively disconnects E from H via the right-hand side of the graph.

    All identified paths between E and H are broken by this set of 4 edges. No smaller set of edges would achieve this, as we found 4 edge-disjoint paths.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(E, H), (F, H), (G, H), (M, J)]

Image name: Tu_task3_gt2_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 5
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are G and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between G and I.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of G and I to understand the immediate connections:
*   Neighbors of G: H, K, F, X
*   Neighbors of I: H, P, J, C

Next, I systematically explore all possible paths from G to I. I can categorize these paths based on the first node encountered from G that leads towards I, or the last node before I.

Let's consider the "bottlenecks" or "channels" through which all paths from G to I must pass.

**Channel 1: Paths through node H**
*   Path 1: G - H - I
*   Path 2: G - H - C - I

To cut all paths in Channel 1, I can remove either edge (G, H) or edge (H, I). Removing (G, H) would prevent G from reaching H, thus breaking both Path 1 and Path 2. Removing (H, I) would prevent H from reaching I, also breaking both Path 1 and Path 2. Either option requires 1 edge.

**Channel 2: Paths through node F (and subsequently P)**
All other paths from G to I must go through one of G's other neighbors (K, F, X) and eventually reach F. From F, the only way to reach I is via node P.
Let's list these paths:
*   Path 3: G - F - P - I
*   Path 4: G - F - P - J - I
*   Path 5: G - K - F - P - I
*   Path 6: G - K - F - P - J - I
*   Path 7: G - X - F - P - I
*   Path 8: G - X - F - P - J - I

To cut all paths in Channel 2, I need to ensure that F cannot reach I.
From F, the connections to I are:
*   F - P - I (via edge (P, I))
*   F - P - J - I (via edge (P, J) and (J, I))

The most efficient way to cut all paths originating from F and going to I is to remove the edge (F, P). This single edge removal breaks the connection from F to P, thereby severing all paths that rely on F-P-I or F-P-J-I. An alternative would be to remove (P, I) AND (J, I), which would require 2 edges, making (F, P) the minimal choice for this channel.

**Combining the cuts:**
To completely disconnect G from I, I must cut at least one edge from Channel 1 AND at least one edge from Channel 2.

*   Minimum edges for Channel 1: 1 (e.g., (G, H))
*   Minimum edges for Channel 2: 1 (e.g., (F, P))

Therefore, the total minimum number of edges to remove is 1 + 1 = 2.

**Verification of a cut of size 2:**
Let's choose the cut set: [(G, H), (F, P)].
1.  Removing (G, H) breaks all paths that go G -> H -> ... -> I (Paths 1 and 2).

Since no single edge removal disconnects G and I, the minimum number of edges is indeed 2.

    *   Set 3: {G, K, J, P, O}
    These three sets are indeed disjoint. This means there are at least three vertex-disjoint paths between H and N.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem**: According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes (the vertex connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them. Since I found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3.

5.  **Propose a Cut Set of Size 3**: To disconnect H and N, I can choose one intermediate node from each of these disjoint paths. For example, I can choose B from Path 1, I from Path 2, and G from Path 3. This gives a potential cut set: [B, I, G].

    Since all paths from H to N must start with H-B, H-G, or H-I, and removing B, G, and I severs all these initial branches, no path can remain.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and J (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect E and J.

1.  **Identify the neighbors of the target nodes:**
    *   Neighbors of E: F, L, M, I, B
    *   Neighbors of J: M, D, P, I

2.  **Look for direct connections and common neighbors:**
    *   E and J share common neighbors M and I. This means there are direct paths E-M-J and E-I-J.

3.  **Consider a cut set by isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   To isolate J, we could remove all edges connected to it: (J, M), (J, D), (J, P), (J, I). This would be a cut of size 4.
    *   To isolate E, we could remove all edges connected to it: (E, F), (E, L), (E, M), (E, I), (E, B). This would be a cut of size 5.
    *   Since a cut of size 4 is possible, we know the minimum is at most 4.

4.  **Try to find a smaller cut (e.g., size 3):**
    Let's focus on the edges directly connected to J. If we remove a subset of these edges, can we disconnect J from E?
    Consider the cut set: [(J, M), (J, P), (J, I)].
    *   If we remove (J, M), any path from E to J that goes through M (e.g., E-M-J, E-L-M-J) is broken at J.
    *   If we remove (J, I), any path from E to J that goes through I (e.g., E-I-J, E-B-H-I-J, E-F-G-H-I-J, E-I-P-J, etc.) is broken at J.
    *   If we remove (J, P), any path from E to J that goes through P (e.g., E-B-C-O-P-J, E-B-C-N-O-P-J) is broken at J.

    After removing these three edges, J is still connected to D via (J, D). We need to check if there's any path from E to D.
    *   The neighbors of E are {F, L, M, I, B}.
    *   The neighbors of D are {J, P}.
    *   Since P is disconnected from J by removing (J,P), and D is only connected to J and P, there is no path from E to D.
    *   Therefore, removing [(J, M), (J, P), (J, I)] successfully disconnects E and J. This is a cut of size 3.

5.  **Verify if a cut of size 2 is possible:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, we need to show that there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J.
    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J (Edges: (E, M), (M, J))
    *   **Path 2:** E - I - J (Edges: (E, I), (I, J))
    *   **Path 3:** E - B - C - O - P - J (Edges: (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J))

    *   Edges in Path 2: {(E, I), (I, J)}
    *   Edges in Path 3: {(E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)}
    All three sets of edges are distinct, meaning these are indeed three edge-disjoint paths.
    According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size must be at least 3.

6.  **Conclusion:**

Step-by-step reasoning.

First, I will try to identify vertex-disjoint paths between X and C. If I can find `k` vertex-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, at least `k` nodes must be removed to disconnect X and C.

Let's trace paths from X to C:

1.  **Path 1: X - F - E - B - C**
    *   Intermediate nodes: {F, E, B}

2.  **Path 2: X - G - H - I - C**
    *   Intermediate nodes: {G, H, I}

3.  **Path 3: X - K - J - P - O - C**
    *   Intermediate nodes: {K, J, P, O}

Let's check if these three paths are vertex-disjoint (excluding the source X and target C):
*   Intermediate nodes of Path 1: {F, E, B}
*   Intermediate nodes of Path 2: {G, H, I}
*   Intermediate nodes of Path 3: {K, J, P, O}

All three sets of intermediate nodes are distinct and do not overlap. Therefore, these are three vertex-disjoint paths between X and C.

According to Menger's Theorem, if there are `k` vertex-disjoint paths between two nodes, then at least `k` nodes must be removed to disconnect them. In this case, since I found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3.

Now, I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects X and C.
A straightforward approach is to remove all direct neighbors of X. The neighbors of X are F, G, and K.

Let's verify if removing nodes [F, G, K] disconnects X from C:
*   If F is removed, all paths starting with X-F are broken.
*   If G is removed, all paths starting with X-G are broken.
*   If K is removed, all paths starting with X-K are broken.

Since F, G, and K are the only nodes directly connected to X, removing them will isolate X from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from X to C.

Since we established that at least 3 nodes are required, and we found a set of 3 nodes ([F, G, K]) that successfully disconnects X from C, this is the minimum number of nodes.

    *   If G and B are removed: Paths through I still exist, for example, E-L-M-J-P-I-H.
    *   If G and I are removed: Paths through B still exist, for example, E-B-H.
    *   If B and I are removed: Paths through G still exist, for example, E-G-H.

    *   Path 2: E - B - H (Nodes: B)
    *   Path 3: E - L - M - J - P - I - H (Nodes: L, M, J, P, I)
    Since these three paths are node-disjoint (they do not share any intermediate nodes), by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect E and H.
8.  **Conclusion:** Since 3 nodes are necessary (as shown by the node-disjoint paths and the analysis of single/double node removals) and 3 nodes (G, I, B) are sufficient, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

First, I will identify edge-disjoint paths from G to I.

1.  **Path 1: G - H - I**
    Edges: (G,H), (H,I)

2.  **Path 2: G - F - E - I**
    Edges: (G,F), (F,E), (E,I)
    Let's check if Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint.
    Edges in Path 1: {(G,H), (H,I)}
    Edges in Path 2: {(G,F), (F,E), (E,I)}
    There are no common edges between Path 1 and Path 2. Thus, they are edge-disjoint. This implies that at least 2 edges must be removed.

3.  **Path 3: G - K - F - H - B - I**
    Edges: (G,K), (K,F), (F,H), (H,B), (B,I)
    Let's check if Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.
    Edges in Path 1: {(G,H), (H,I)}
    Edges in Path 2: {(G,F), (F,E), (E,I)}
    Edges in Path 3: {(G,K), (K,F), (F,H), (H,B), (B,I)}
    *   Comparing Path 3 with Path 1: No common edges.
    *   Comparing Path 3 with Path 2: No common edges.
    All three paths (Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3) are edge-disjoint. This means that at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect G from I.

Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. Now, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects G from I.

Consider the following set of edges:
**Cut Set: {(G,H), (F,E), (F,H)}**

Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects G from I:
*   **Edge (G,H):** This edge is part of Path 1 (G-H-I) and also a critical link from G to H.
*   **Edge (F,E):** This edge is part of Path 2 (G-F-E-I) and a critical link from F to E.
*   **Edge (F,H):** This edge is part of Path 3 (G-K-F-H-B-I) and a critical link from F to H.

After removing these three edges:
1.  The direct connection G-H is broken.
2.  Any path from G that goes through F (e.g., G-F-...) can no longer reach E (because (F,E) is removed) or H (because (F,H) is removed).
3.  Since nodes K and X are only connected to G and F (and each other), any path starting G-K... or G-X... must eventually pass through F. From F, as established, there are no paths to E or H.

Therefore, after removing these three edges, the component containing G (which includes G, X, K, F) is completely disconnected from the rest of the graph, including I.

Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and I found a cut set of 3 edges, this is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(F,E), (F,H), (G,H)]

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from B. I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of H and B:
Neighbors of H: G, F, B, I, E
Neighbors of B: H, E, I, C, N

Now, I will systematically find edge-disjoint paths between H and B.

1.  **Path 1: H - B**
    Edges: `{(H, B)}`

2.  **Path 2: H - I - B**
    Edges: `{(H, I), (I, B)}`
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

3.  **Path 3: H - E - B**
    Edges: `{(H, E), (E, B)}`
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

Now, I need to find a fourth path that does not use any of the edges from Paths 1, 2, or 3: `{(H, B), (H, I), (I, B), (H, E), (E, B)}`.
From H, the remaining unused direct connections are (H, G) and (H, F).
To B, the remaining unused direct connections are (B, C) and (B, N).

4.  **Path 4: H - G - F - E - I - C - B**
    Edges: `{(H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, I), (I, C), (C, B)}`
    Let's check if this path is edge-disjoint from the previous three:
    *   It does not use (H, B) (from P1).
    *   It uses (E, I) and (I, C), but not (H, I) or (I, B) (from P2). So, it's disjoint from P2.
    *   It uses (F, E) and (E, I), but not (H, E) or (E, B) (from P3). So, it's disjoint from P3.
    This is a valid fourth edge-disjoint path.

Since I have found 4 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 4.

Next, I will attempt to find a cut set of 4 edges that disconnects H from B. A common strategy is to cut all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes, except for those that are part of a cycle or lead to a dead end.

Consider cutting the edges directly connected to H, except for (H, F), which leads to a more complex part of the graph.
The edges connected to H are: (H, B), (H, I), (H, E), (H, G), (H, F).
Let's try removing the following 4 edges: `[(H, B), (H, I), (H, E), (H, G)]`.

Now, let's verify if H is disconnected from B after removing these 4 edges:
*   **Direct paths:**
    *   H - B is cut by (H, B).
    *   H - I - B is cut by (H, I).
    *   H - E - B is cut by (H, E).
    *   H - I - C - B is cut by (H, I).
    *   H - E - I - B is cut by (H, E) or (H, I).
    *   H - E - I - C - B is cut by (H, E) or (H, I).

*   **Paths through G:** Any path starting H - G... is cut by (H, G). This includes paths like H - G - F - E - B, H - G - F - E - I - B, H - G - F - E - I - C - B, and longer paths through L, M, J, P, O, N.

*   **Paths through F:** After removing the specified edges, H is only connected to F.

    *   F -> G: H cannot reach G because (H, G) is removed. So, H - F - G... paths are broken.
    *   F -> K or F -> X: These nodes eventually lead to G, which is disconnected from H.
    *   F -> E: H cannot reach E because (H, E) is removed. So, H - F - E... paths are broken.

Since all paths from H to B are broken by the removal of these 4 edges, and we have established that the minimum cut size is at least 4, this set of 4 edges is indeed the minimum.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F and D. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect F from D.

1.  **Identify direct connections to target node D:**
    Node D is directly connected to nodes P, J, and O. This means any path from F to D must pass through one of these three nodes and use the corresponding edge: (P, D), (J, D), or (O, D).

2.  **Consider removing edges directly connected to D:**
    If we remove all three edges connected to D – (P, D), (J, D), and (O, D) – then D will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from F. This constitutes a valid cut set of size 3.

3.  **Check for minimality (can we do it with 2 edges?):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, we must show that removing any two edges is not sufficient to disconnect F from D. Let's test all combinations of removing two edges from the set {(P, D), (J, D), (O, D)}:

    *   **Case 1: Remove (P, D) and (J, D).**
        If these two edges are removed, any remaining path from F to D must go through node O and use the edge (O, D). Let's check if a path from F to O exists:
        Path: F - E - B - C - O.
        Since this path exists, removing (P, D) and (J, D) is not sufficient, as the path F - E - B - C - O - D would still connect F and D.

    *   **Case 2: Remove (P, D) and (O, D).**
        If these two edges are removed, any remaining path from F to D must go through node J and use the edge (J, D). Let's check if a path from F to J exists:
        Path: F - E - M - J.
        Since this path exists, removing (P, D) and (O, D) is not sufficient, as the path F - E - M - J - D would still connect F and D.

    *   **Case 3: Remove (J, D) and (O, D).**
        If these two edges are removed, any remaining path from F to D must go through node P and use the edge (P, D). Let's check if a path from F to P exists:
        Path: F - G - H - I - P.
        Since this path exists, removing (J, D) and (O, D) is not sufficient, as the path F - G - H - I - P - D would still connect F and D.

4.  **Conclusion on minimality:**
    Since removing any two of the edges directly connected to D is not sufficient to disconnect F from D, we must remove at least three edges. The set of three edges [(P, D), (J, D), (O, D)] successfully disconnects D from F. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(P, D), (J, D), (O, D)]

3.  **Hypothesize a cut set:** If we remove all edges directly connected to H, H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from N. This set of edges is [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]. This cut set has a size of 3.

4.  **Verify the hypothesized cut set:** By removing (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H), no path can reach H from any other node, including N. Therefore, this is a valid cut set.

5.  **Attempt to find a smaller cut set (size 1 or 2):**
    *   **Size 1:** It's impossible to disconnect H from the graph by removing only one edge, as H has three distinct connections. Removing one would leave at least two other paths to H.
    *   **Size 2:** We need to check if removing any two edges can disconnect N from H. Let's consider removing any two edges from the set of edges directly connected to H:

        *   **Remove (G, H) and (I, H):** A path still exists from N to H via B. For example, N - B - H is still a valid path. Also, N - O - C - B - H is still a valid path.

6.  **Conclusion on minimum size:** Since removing any two edges directly connected to H (which are the "entry points" to H from the rest of the graph) still leaves at least one path, a cut set of size 2 is not sufficient. Therefore, the minimum number of edges required is at least 3. As we found a valid cut set of size 3, this is the minimum.

Final Answer:

Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 5
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that, when removed, will completely disconnect E from J.

1.  **Identify potential paths:** I will look for distinct paths from E to J.
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - K - J
    *   **Path 3:** E - G - H - I - J
    *   **Path 4:** E - B - C - O - P - J

2.  **Check for edge-disjointness of these paths:**
    *   **Path 1 edges:** (E, M), (M, J)
    *   **Path 2 edges:** (E, F), (F, K), (K, J)
    *   **Path 3 edges:** (E, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J)
    *   **Path 4 edges:** (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)

    Let's examine if any edges are shared between these four paths:
    *   The first edges from E are (E, M), (E, F), (E, G), (E, B). These are all distinct.
    *   The last edges to J are (M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J). These are all distinct.
    *   All intermediate edges (F, K), (G, H), (H, I), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P) are also distinct from each other and from the start/end edges.

    Since these four paths are edge-disjoint, according to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect E and J must be at least 4.

3.  **Identify a candidate cut set of size 4:**
    To disconnect E from J, we need to ensure no path exists. A straightforward way to achieve this, given the identified edge-disjoint paths, is to remove one edge from each path. The edges directly connected to J from these paths are good candidates for a minimal cut set.
    *   From Path 1: (M, J)
    *   From Path 2: (K, J)
    *   From Path 3: (I, J)
    *   From Path 4: (P, J)

    This gives us a candidate cut set: `[(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J)]`. This set has 4 edges.

4.  **Verify the cut set:**
    If we remove these four edges:
    *   (M, J): Any path from E to J that goes through M (e.g., E-M-J, E-L-M-J, E-F-M-J) is broken.
    *   (K, J): Any path from E to J that goes through K (e.g., E-F-K-J, E-F-X-K-J, E-G-F-K-J) is broken.
    *   (I, J): Any path from E to J that goes through I (e.g., E-G-H-I-J, E-B-C-I-J) is broken.
    *   (P, J): Any path from E to J that goes through P (e.g., E-B-C-O-P-J, E-B-N-Y-O-P-J) is broken.

    The node D is connected to J and P. Since (P, J) is removed, any path from E to J via P is broken. D itself is not an intermediate node for paths from E to J, as it only connects to J and P. Therefore, removing these four edges successfully disconnects E from J.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    We have identified 4 edge-disjoint paths, which means at least 4 edges must be removed. We have also found a cut set of exactly 4 edges that successfully disconnects E from J. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J)]

Image name: POGBb_task2_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 5
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are O and H. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect O from H. I cannot remove O or H.

2.  **Explore paths from O to H:** I will systematically list all distinct paths from O to H.
    *   **Paths starting from O's neighbor N:**
        *   Path 1: O - N - B - H
    *   **Paths starting from O's neighbor C:**
        *   Path 2: O - C - B - H
        *   Path 3: O - C - I - H
    *   **Paths starting from O's neighbor P:**
        *   Path 4: O - P - I - H
        *   Path 5: O - P - J - M - E - B - H
        *   Path 6: O - P - J - M - E - F - K - G - H
        *   Path 7: O - P - J - M - L - E - F - K - G - H
    *   **Paths starting from O's neighbor Y:**
        *   Path 8: O - Y - N - B - H (This path uses N, similar to Path 1 but with Y as an extra step)

3.  **Identify potential cut sets:** A cut set is a set of nodes whose removal disconnects the two target nodes. The goal is to find the *minimum* size cut set.

4.  **Check for a cut set of size 1:**
    *   If I remove any single node (e.g., N, C, P, B, I, G, E, M, J, L, F, K, X, D, Y), there will always be other paths remaining. For example, removing N still leaves paths through C and P. Removing B still leaves paths through I and G. Thus, a single node is not sufficient.

5.  **Check for a cut set of size 2:**
    *   I tried various combinations of two nodes (e.g., [N, C], [N, P], [C, P], [B, I], [B, G], [I, G]). In each case, I found at least one path that remained connected. For instance, if I remove [B, I], paths like O - P - J - M - E - F - K - G - H still exist. If I remove [N, C], paths like O - P - I - H still exist. This suggests that a cut set of size 2 is unlikely.

6.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (intuition):** Menger's theorem states that the minimum number of vertices to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices (O and H) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them. Let's try to find vertex-disjoint paths (excluding O and H).
    *   **Path A:** O - N - B - H (Intermediate nodes: {N, B})
    *   **Path B:** O - C - I - H (Intermediate nodes: {C, I})
    *   **Path C:** O - P - J - M - E - F - K - G - H (Intermediate nodes: {P, J, M, E, F, K, G})

    These three paths (Path A, Path B, Path C) are vertex-disjoint in their intermediate nodes.
    *   {N, B} has no common nodes with {C, I}.
    *   {N, B} has no common nodes with {P, J, M, E, F, K, G}.

        *   J -> M -> L -> E -> B -> C -> H -> F

        *   All these paths pass through E.

            *   J -> D -> P -> O -> N -> B -> C -> H -> F

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are O and H. The task is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect O from H. This is a minimum cut problem, which by the Max-Flow Min-Cut theorem, is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between O and H.

First, let's identify all direct neighbors of O and H to understand the immediate connections:
Neighbors of O: P, Y, N, M
Neighbors of H: I, C, X, G

Now, let's try to find edge-disjoint paths from O to H. An edge-disjoint path means that no two paths share any common edge.

1.  **Path 1 (via P and I):**
    O - P - D - J - I - H
    Edges: (O,P), (P,D), (D,J), (J,I), (I,H)

2.  **Path 2 (via N and C):**
    O - N - B - C - H
    Edges: (O,N), (N,B), (B,C), (C,H)
    (This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 as it uses different initial edges from O and different intermediate nodes/edges.)

3.  **Path 3 (via M, L, E, F, and X):**
    O - M - L - E - F - X - H
    Edges: (O,M), (M,L), (L,E), (E,F), (F,X), (X,H)
    (This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2. It uses a different initial edge from O and distinct intermediate edges.)

We have found three edge-disjoint paths between O and H. According to the Max-Flow Min-Cut theorem, this implies that the minimum number of edges required to disconnect O from H is at least 3.

Now, we need to find a cut set of 3 edges that actually disconnects O and H. A cut set must contain at least one edge from every path between O and H.

Let's propose a cut set based on these three edge-disjoint paths, choosing one edge from each path that seems critical:
- From Path 1: (I, H)
- From Path 2: (C, H)
- From Path 3: (E, F)

Let's verify if removing these three edges, **Cut Set = [(I, H), (C, H), (E, F)]**, completely disconnects O from H.

1.  **Removing (I, H):** This edge is the direct connection from node I to H. Any path that reaches H via node I will be broken. This includes paths like O-P-D-J-I-H, O-M-J-I-H, O-Y-P-D-J-I-H.

2.  **Removing (C, H):** This edge is the direct connection from node C to H. Any path that reaches H via node C will be broken. This includes paths like O-P-Y-N-C-H, O-N-C-H, O-N-B-C-H, O-M-L-E-B-C-H, O-Y-N-C-H, O-Y-N-B-C-H.

3.  **Removing (E, F):** This edge is a critical link in the lower part of the graph. Let's analyze paths that could reach H via nodes X or G (since (I,H) and (C,H) are already cut).
    -   Nodes X and G are directly connected to H.
    -   To reach X or G from the "O side" of the graph, one must pass through nodes F or K.
    -   Node F is connected to E, X, K.
    -   Node K is connected to F, X, G.
    -   Node E is connected to B, F, L.
    -   Crucially, to reach F (and subsequently X, K, G) from the "O side", all paths must pass through node E. From E, the only way to reach F is via the edge (E, F).
    -   Since (E, F) is removed, no path from O can reach F.
    -   As F is the gateway to X, K, and G from the E-side of the graph, cutting (E, F) effectively isolates X, K, and G from any path originating from O.
    -   Therefore, no path can reach H via X or G.

Since all paths to H (via I, C, X, or G) are broken by the removal of these three edges, O and H are completely disconnected.
As we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and we found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum number of edges to remove.

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(I, H), (C, H), (E, F)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect B from H.

1.  **Identify direct connections to H:** Node H is directly connected to three other nodes: B, G, and I. This means any path from B to H must eventually use one of these three edges: (B, H), (G, H), or (I, H).

2.  **Consider a cut set of these direct edges:** If we remove all three edges connected to H, namely (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H), then H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path will exist between B and H. This gives us a potential cut set of size 3: `[(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]`.

3.  **Check for edge-disjoint paths (Menger's Theorem):** To confirm if 3 is the minimum, I will try to find three edge-disjoint paths between B and H. If I can find three such paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to cut is at least 3.
    *   **Path 1:** B - H
        *   Edges: `{(B, H)}`
    *   **Path 2:** B - C - I - H
        *   Edges: `{(B, C), (C, I), (I, H)}`
    *   **Path 3:** B - E - F - X - G - H
        *   Edges: `{(B, E), (E, F), (F, X), (X, G), (G, H)}`

    Let's verify if these paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   Path 1 uses (B, H).
    *   Path 2 uses (B, C), (C, I), (I, H). None of these edges are in Path 1.
    *   Path 3 uses (B, E), (E, F), (F, X), (X, G), (G, H). None of these edges are in Path 1 or Path 2.
    
    Since these three paths share no common edges, they are indeed edge-disjoint.

4.  **Conclusion on minimum cut size:** Because there are 3 edge-disjoint paths from B to H, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them must be at least 3. Since we found a cut set of size 3 (by removing the edges directly connected to H), this is the minimum possible.

5.  **Final verification of the chosen cut set:**
    *   Removing (B, H) breaks the direct connection.
    *   Removing (G, H) breaks all paths that would reach H via node G (e.g., B-E-F-X-G-H, B-E-F-K-G-H).
    *   Removing (I, H) breaks all paths that would reach H via node I (e.g., B-C-I-H, B-N-C-I-H, B-E-M-J-D-P-I-H).
    With these three edges removed, H has no remaining connections to the rest of the graph, thus ensuring no path exists between B and H.

The minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

    Let's identify distinct paths from J to E:
    *   **Path 1 (via M):** J - M - E. This is a direct path.
    *   **Path 2 (via P, O, B):** J - P - O - N - B - E. (There are variations like J-D-P-O-N-B-E, J-P-O-Y-N-B-E, J-P-O-C-B-E, etc., but they all share P, O, and B as critical nodes in this general route).
    *   **Path 3 (via I, H, F):** J - I - H - X - K - F - E. (There are variations like J-I-H-G-X-K-F-E, J-I-H-G-K-F-E, etc., but they all share I, H, and F as critical nodes in this general route).
    *   **Path 4 (via I, H, C, B):** J - I - H - C - B - E. (This path uses I, H, C, and B. Note that B is also used in Path 2).

    *   **P_A:** J - M - E. (Intermediate node: {M})
    *   **P_B:** J - P - O - N - B - E. (Intermediate nodes: {P, O, N, B})
    *   **P_C:** J - I - H - X - K - F - E. (Intermediate nodes: {I, H, X, K, F})

    *   The set of intermediate nodes for P_A is {M}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for P_B is {P, O, N, B}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for P_C is {I, H, X, K, F}.

    These three sets are mutually disjoint. Therefore, we have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between J and E. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect J and E is at least 3.

5.  **Identify a Cut Set of Size 3:**
    Since the minimum cut size is at least 3, I will now look for a cut set of size 3.
    Consider the direct neighbors of E: {L, B, F, M}. Any path from J to E must pass through one of these nodes (or M via L).
    *   If a path uses the edge (E, L), it must come from M (J-M-L-E). So it passes through M.
    *   If a path uses the edge (E, B), it passes through B.
    *   If a path uses the edge (E, F), it passes through F.
    *   If a path uses the edge (E, M), it passes through M.

    If I remove these three nodes:
    *   **Remove M:** All paths involving M (J-M-E, J-M-L-E) are broken.
    *   **Remove B:** All paths involving B (e.g., J-P-O-N-B-E, J-I-H-C-B-E) are broken.
    *   **Remove F:** All paths involving F (e.g., J-I-H-X-K-F-E) are broken.

    The cut set [M, B, F] has a size of 3. Since we established that the minimum cut size is at least 3, and we found a cut set of size 3, this is indeed the minimum.

    Let's consider removing E (from Path 1) and H (from Path 2).

    *   **If E is removed:**
        *   All paths that go through E are broken. This includes M-E-B, M-L-E-..., M-E-C-..., M-E-F-...
        *   M's remaining connections are to L and K.
        *   L is only connected to M and E. If E is removed, L is isolated from the rest of the graph (except M). So, any path M-L-... is broken.
        *   B's remaining connections are to H, C, N.

    *   **After removing E, remaining paths from M to B must go through K:**
        *   M - K - X - G - H - B
        *   M - K - F - G - H - B
        *   M - K - F - X - G - H - B
        All these remaining paths from M (via K) to B converge through nodes G and H to reach B.

        *   The paths M-K-X-G-H-B, M-K-F-G-H-B, and M-K-F-X-G-H-B are all broken because H is removed. Node G is no longer connected to B's side.

7.  **Conclusion:** Removing both E and H successfully disconnects M from B. Since we established that at least 2 nodes must be removed, and we found a set of 2 nodes that achieves this, [E, H] is a minimum cut set.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, H]

Let's try to identify a minimal cut set. A cut set of size 1 is generally unlikely in a well-connected graph like this, as it would imply a single bridge edge. Given the multiple paths, it's highly improbable.

Let's consider potential cut sets of size 2. This means we are looking for two edges whose removal would break all paths between B and M.

Consider the edges (H, E) and (J, M).
Let's analyze the impact of removing these two edges:

1.  **Impact of removing (J, M):**
    *   Any path that directly uses the edge (J, M) to reach M will be broken. This includes paths like B-H-I-J-M, B-C-I-J-M, B-N-O-P-J-M, and any other path that ends with ...-J-M.
    *   After removing (J, M), M can only be reached via edges (E, M) or (L, M).

2.  **Impact of removing (H, E):**
    *   Any path that directly uses the edge (H, E) will be broken. This includes paths like B-H-E-M and B-H-E-L-M.
    *   More importantly, let's analyze the reachability of nodes E and L from B after removing (H, E):
        *   **Reachability of E from B:**
            *   Paths from B to E:
                *   B-H-E (broken by (H, E))
                *   B-C-I-H-E (broken by (H, E))
                *   B-N-C-I-H-E (broken by (H, E))
                *   B-N-O-P-I-H-E (broken by (H, E))
            *   Upon careful inspection, all paths from B to E must pass through the edge (H, E). Therefore, if (H, E) is removed, node E becomes unreachable from B.
        *   **Reachability of L from B:**
            *   Paths from B to L:
                *   B-H-E-L (broken by (H, E))
                *   B-C-I-H-E-L (broken by (H, E))
                *   B-N-C-I-H-E-L (broken by (H, E))
                *   B-N-O-P-I-H-E-L (broken by (H, E))
            *   Similarly, all paths from B to L must pass through the edge (H, E). Therefore, if (H, E) is removed, node L becomes unreachable from B.

**Conclusion after removing (H, E) and (J, M):**
*   Since (J, M) is removed, M cannot be reached via J.
*   Since (H, E) is removed, E is unreachable from B. Therefore, M cannot be reached via E (i.e., no path B-...-E-M).
*   Since (H, E) is removed, L is unreachable from B. Therefore, M cannot be reached via L (i.e., no path B-...-L-M).

Because M cannot be reached through any of its direct neighbors (E, J, L) from B, nodes B and M are completely disconnected.

This cut set consists of 2 edges: (H, E) and (J, M).
Since a cut of size 1 is not possible (as there are clearly multiple, non-trivial paths between B and M), 2 is the minimum number of edges.

2.  **Understand the goal:** Find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from I.
3.  **Strategy: Menger's Theorem (Edge Version):** The minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes. If I can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut is at least *k*. If I can then find a cut set of size *k*, that is the minimum.

4.  **Find edge-disjoint paths between G and I:**
    *   **Path 1 (P1):** G - E - I
        *   Edges: {(G, E), (E, I)}
    *   **Path 2 (P2):** G - H - I
        *   Edges: {(G, H), (H, I)}
        *   P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint as they use different direct connections from G and to I.
    *   **Path 3 (P3):** G - F - E - M - J - I
        *   Edges: {(G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, I)}
        *   Let's check if P3 is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2:
            *   Edges in P1: {(G, E), (E, I)}
            *   Edges in P2: {(G, H), (H, I)}
            *   Edges in P3: {(G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, I)}
            *   There are no common edges between P1, P2, and P3. For example, P3 uses (G,F) instead of (G,E) or (G,H), and it uses (J,I) instead of (E,I) or (H,I).

5.  **Conclusion from edge-disjoint paths:** Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and I, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 3.

6.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** Now, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects G from I.
    *   Consider the edges that connect G's immediate neighborhood to the rest of the graph, specifically towards I.
    *   The direct connections from G to E and H are (G,E) and (G,H).
    *   Another way for G to reach E is through F: G - F - E. The edge (F,E) is crucial here.
    *   Let's propose the cut set: **[(G,E), (G,H), (F,E)]**.

7.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   If (G,E) is removed, G cannot directly reach E.
    *   If (G,H) is removed, G cannot directly reach H.
    *   If (F,E) is removed, G cannot reach E via F (e.g., G-F-E is broken, G-K-F-E is broken, G-X-F-E is broken).
    *   After removing these three edges, the node G (along with K, X, F, which are connected to G but not to E or H anymore) is completely isolated from nodes E and H.

        *   Any path G-...-H-...-I is broken because G cannot reach H.
        *   Any path G-...-J-...-I, G-...-P-...-I, G-...-C-...-I must eventually pass through E or H to connect to G's side of the graph. For example, to reach J, P, or C from G, one must traverse E or H (e.g., G-E-M-J, G-H-B-C). Since G is disconnected from E and H, it is disconnected from J, P, and C, and thus from I.

8.  **Conclusion:** The cut set [(G,E), (G,H), (F,E)] successfully disconnects G from I, and its size is 3. Since we established that the minimum cut must be at least 3, this cut set is indeed minimal.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node J is connected to: D, P, M, I.
    *   Node E is connected to: B, L, F.

2.  **Consider a simple cut by isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   To isolate J, we would need to remove all edges connected to J: (J,D), (J,P), (J,M), (J,I). This is a cut of size 4.
    *   To isolate E, we would need to remove all edges connected to E: (E,B), (E,L), (E,F). This is a cut of size 3.
    *   This suggests that the minimum cut might be 3.

3.  **Attempt to find a smaller cut (e.g., size 1 or 2):**
    *   **Size 1:** It's clear that removing any single edge will not disconnect J and E, as there are multiple distinct paths between them. For example, removing (E,B) would still leave paths like J-M-L-E or J-I-G-F-E.
    *   **Size 2:** Let's test if removing any two edges connected to E would work.
        *   If we remove (E,B) and (E,L), paths to E via F would still exist (e.g., J-I-G-F-E).
        *   If we remove (E,B) and (E,F), paths to E via L would still exist (e.g., J-M-L-E).
        *   If we remove (E,L) and (E,F), paths to E via B would still exist (e.g., J-I-C-B-E).
        *   Since removing any two edges directly connected to E is insufficient, a cut of size 2 is unlikely to be found by targeting E's immediate connections. It's also generally difficult to find a size 2 cut in a complex graph by inspection without a systematic approach.

4.  **Use Menger's Theorem (or intuition for edge-disjoint paths):**
    Menger's theorem states that the minimum number of edges in an edge cut separating two non-adjacent vertices is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. If we can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut is at least 3.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths from J to E:
    *   **Path 1:** J - M - L - E
        Edges: {(J,M), (M,L), (L,E)}
    *   **Path 2:** J - D - P - O - N - B - E
        Edges: {(J,D), (D,P), (P,O), (O,N), (N,B), (B,E)}
    *   **Path 3:** J - I - H - X - F - E
        Edges: {(J,I), (I,H), (H,X), (X,F), (F,E)}

    Let's verify if these paths are edge-disjoint (i.e., they do not share any common edges):
    *   Edges from Path 1: (J,M), (M,L), (L,E)
    *   Edges from Path 2: (J,D), (D,P), (P,O), (O,N), (N,B), (B,E)
    *   Edges from Path 3: (J,I), (I,H), (H,X), (X,F), (F,E)

    Upon inspection, all edges in these three paths are distinct. For example, the starting edges (J,M), (J,D), (J,I) are distinct. The ending edges (L,E), (B,E), (F,E) are distinct. All intermediate edges are also distinct.
    Therefore, we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between J and E.

5.  **Conclusion based on Menger's Theorem:**
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect J and E is at least 3.
    We already identified a cut set of size 3: [(E,B), (E,L), (E,F)]. This set of edges disconnects E from all other nodes, and thus from J.

6.  **Final verification:**
    *   Removing (E,B) breaks all paths to E via B.
    *   Removing (E,L) breaks all paths to E via L.
    *   Removing (E,F) breaks all paths to E via F.
    *   Since B, L, and F are the only direct neighbors of E, removing these three edges guarantees that no path exists from J to E.
    *   This cut set has a size of 3, which matches the lower bound established by Menger's theorem.

The minimum number of edges to remove is 3.
