Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to ensure no path exists between E and J.

1.  **Identify direct connections and common neighbors:**
    *   Node E is connected to L, M, P, B.
    *   Node J is connected to M, D, P, I.
    *   Common neighbors of E and J are M and P. This immediately suggests two direct paths: E-M-J and E-P-J.

2.  **Explore all possible paths between E and J:**
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J
    *   **Path 2:** E - P - J
    *   **Path 3 (via B and H):** E - B - H - I - J
    *   **Path 4 (via B and C):** E - B - C - I - J
    *   **Other paths via B and P:** There are paths like E - B - C - O - P - J or E - B - H - G - F - P - J. However, these paths eventually use node P, which is already covered by Path 2 (E-P-J). If we cut (E, P) or (P, J), these paths are also broken.

3.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths:**
    To determine the minimum number of edges to remove (edge connectivity), I can look for the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and J.
    *   **Path A:** E - M - J (Edges: (E, M), (M, J))
    *   **Path B:** E - P - J (Edges: (E, P), (P, J))
    *   **Path C:** E - B - H - I - J (Edges: (E, B), (B, H), (H, I), (I, J))

    Let's check if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   Path A uses (E, M) and (M, J).
    *   Path B uses (E, P) and (P, J).
    *   Path C uses (E, B), (B, H), (H, I), (I, J).
    All edges in Path A, Path B, and Path C are distinct. Therefore, these three paths are edge-disjoint.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:**
    Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect E and J is at least 3.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects E and J.
    Consider the following cut set: [(E, M), (E, P), (I, J)].
    *   Removing (E, M) breaks Path A (E-M-J).
    *   Removing (E, P) breaks Path B (E-P-J) and any other path that uses (E, P) or (P, J) to connect to P from E (e.g., E-B-C-O-P-J).
    *   Removing (I, J) breaks Path C (E-B-H-I-J) and Path 4 (E-B-C-I-J), as both rely on (I, J) as their final connection to J via I.

    With these three edges removed, all identified paths between E and J are broken.
    *   E is now connected to L and B.
    *   J is now connected to M, D, P.
    *   No path from E to M (because (E,M) is cut).
    *   No path from E to P (because (E,P) is cut).
    *   No path from E to I (because even if E reaches I via B, the (I,J) edge is cut).
    Therefore, E and J are disconnected.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. We have also found a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects E and J. Thus, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, M), (E, P), (I, J)]

        *   Paths like N - B - E - F - K - G would be cut by (E, F).
        *   However, paths like N - B - H - G would *not* be cut, as (B, H) is still present.
        Therefore, [(I, H), (E, F)] is not a valid cut set.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from N.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Target Node G is connected to: E, F, H, K.
    *   Target Node N is connected to: B, C, Y.

2.  **Analyze the structure around node N:**
    Node N has three direct connections: (B, N), (C, N), and (Y, N). Any path from G to N must traverse one of these three edges.

3.  **Consider a cut set by removing edges directly connected to N:**
    If we remove the edges (B, N), (C, N), and (Y, N), node N will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between G and N. This set contains 3 edges.

4.  **Determine if a smaller cut (e.g., 1 or 2 edges) is possible:**
    *   **Can we disconnect G and N by removing 1 edge?** No, because N has three distinct incoming edges. Removing one would leave at least two other paths to N. For example, removing (B, N) would still leave paths via (C, N) and (Y, N).
    *   **Can we disconnect G and N by removing 2 edges?** Let's test all combinations of removing two edges directly connected to N:
        *   If we remove (B, N) and (C, N): A path to N still exists via Y (e.g., G - H - I - O - Y - N).
        *   If we remove (B, N) and (Y, N): A path to N still exists via C (e.g., G - H - I - C - N).
        *   If we remove (C, N) and (Y, N): A path to N still exists via B (e.g., G - H - B - N).
    Since removing any two edges directly connected to N leaves at least one path to N, a cut of size 2 is not sufficient if we only consider edges directly connected to N.

5.  **Consider alternative cut sets not directly connected to N:**
    We need to ensure that all paths leading to B, C, and Y (which are the predecessors of N) are cut.
    Let's list the edges that feed into B, C, and Y from the "G-side" of the graph:
    *   To B: (H, B), (E, B)
    *   To C: (I, C), (O, C)
    *   To Y: (O, Y)

    If we try to select 2 edges from this larger set, for example:
    *   Removing (H, B) and (E, B) would cut all paths to B. However, paths to C (e.g., G - H - I - C - N) and Y (e.g., G - H - I - O - Y - N) would still exist.
    *   Removing (I, C) and (O, Y) would cut paths to C via I and paths to Y via O. However, paths to B (e.g., G - H - B - N) and paths to C via O (e.g., G - H - I - O - C - N) would still exist.

    It becomes clear that to disconnect N, we must effectively cut off all three "entry points" (B, C, Y) from G. Since these three entry points are distinct and have independent paths leading to them (or paths that can be made independent with careful edge selection), it requires at least three edges to sever all connections.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    The minimum number of edges required to disconnect G from N is 3. The simplest and most direct way to achieve this is by removing the three edges directly connected to N.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, N), (C, N), (Y, N)]

    If I remove all edges connected to H, then H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from E. The edges connected to H are (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H). Removing these three edges would disconnect H. This gives a cut set of size 3.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut is possible (e.g., 1 or 2 edges).**
    To prove that 3 is the *minimum* number of edges, I need to find at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H. If I can find 3 such paths, then by Menger's theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect E and H.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - B - H
        *   Edges: (E, B), (B, H)

    *   **Path 2:** E - F - G - H
        *   Edges: (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)

    *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - P - I - H
        *   Edges: (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, H)

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    *   Edges in Path 1: {(E, B), (B, H)}
    *   Edges in Path 2: {(E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}
    *   Edges in Path 3: {(E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, H)}

    Upon inspection, all edges listed across these three paths are distinct. There are no shared edges between Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3.

5.  **Conclusion based on Menger's Theorem:**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.
    The cut set identified in step 2, [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)], consists of exactly 3 edges. This set successfully disconnects H from all other nodes, including E.
    Therefore, 3 is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

    *   X is connected to G and F.

4.  **Identify potential cut sets:**
    Since all paths from N to G must pass through either H or F, removing both H and F would sever all connections.
    *   If H is removed, all paths that use H to reach G (e.g., N-C-H-G, N-I-H-G, N-P-I-H-G) are broken.
    *   If F is removed, all paths that use F to reach G (e.g., N-P-F-G, N-E-P-F-G, N-I-P-F-G, N-K-F-G, N-X-F-G) are broken.

        *   If we remove F, paths through H still exist (e.g., N-C-H-G).

Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are G and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between G and I.

First, I identify all direct connections from G and to I.
Edges connected to G: (G, X), (G, F), (G, H).
Edges connected to I: (H, I), (C, I), (P, I).

Let's consider the possibility of a cut set of size 1.
If I remove any single edge, will G and I be disconnected?
- Removing (G, H): Paths like G-F-E-B-H-I or G-F-E-B-C-I or G-F-E-M-J-P-I would still exist. So, not enough.
- Removing (H, I): Paths like G-H-B-C-I or G-F-E-B-C-I or G-F-E-M-J-P-I would still exist. So, not enough.
- Similarly, removing any other single edge will leave multiple paths.
Therefore, a cut set of size 1 is not possible. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

Now, let's look for a cut set of size 2.
I need to find two edges whose removal breaks all paths from G to I.
Let's analyze the structure of the graph from G's perspective. Any path originating from G must start with one of its direct neighbors: X, F, or H.

Consider the following two edges: (G, H) and (F, E).

1.  **Paths starting with (G, H):**
    If a path from G to I starts with the edge (G, H), then removing (G, H) will immediately break this path. This includes the direct path G-H-I, and any longer paths like G-H-B-C-I, G-H-B-N-C-I, G-H-B-C-O-P-I, etc.

2.  **Paths starting with (G, F):**
    If a path from G to I starts with the edge (G, F), it must then proceed from F. Node F is connected to G, X, K, and E.
    -   F to G is a backtrack.
    -   F to K: From K, the only connections are to X and F. So, G-F-K-X-G forms a cycle and does not lead to I.
    -   F to X: From X, the only connections are to G, K, and F. Again, this leads back to the G-X-K-F cluster and does not provide a new exit point towards I.
    -   F to E: This is the only way for a path starting G-F to leave the {G, X, K, F} cluster and move towards the right side of the graph where I is located. Therefore, any path G-F-... that eventually reaches I must use the edge (F, E). Removing (F, E) will break all such paths.

3.  **Paths starting with (G, X):**
    If a path from G to I starts with the edge (G, X), it must then proceed from X. Node X is connected to G, K, and F.
    -   X to G is a backtrack.
    -   X to K: From K, the only connections are to X and F. So, G-X-K-F. From F, as established above, the only way to reach I is via (F, E).
    -   X to F: From F, the only way to reach I is via (F, E).
    Therefore, any path G-X-... that eventually reaches I must pass through the edge (F, E). Removing (F, E) will break all such paths.

Combining these observations:
-   All paths from G to I that go through H are cut by removing (G, H).
-   All paths from G to I that go through F or X are cut by removing (F, E), because (F, E) is the only exit from the {G, X, K, F} cluster towards the rest of the graph leading to I.

Thus, removing the two edges (G, H) and (F, E) will completely disconnect G from I.
Since we established that a cut of size 1 is not possible, and we found a cut of size 2, this is the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(G, H), (F, E)]

Neighbors of O: {P, Y, N, C, M}
Neighbors of H: {I, C, X, G}

I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between O and H. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

Let's find vertex-disjoint paths:

1.  **Path 1: O - C - H**
    This is a direct path of length 2. The nodes involved are O, C, H.

2.  **Path 2: O - M - J - I - H**
    Let's check if this path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1. The nodes involved are O, M, J, I, H. None of these nodes (M, J, I) are C. So, Path 1 and Path 2 are vertex-disjoint.

3.  **Path 3: O - N - B - E - F - X - H**

    *   From Path 2 (O-M-J-I-H): None of N, B, E, F, X are M, J, or I. So, Path 3 is disjoint from Path 2.
    Therefore, Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are all vertex-disjoint.

Since I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between O and H, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 3 nodes that successfully disconnects O and H.

*   From Path 1 (O - C - H), I can choose node **C**.
*   From Path 2 (O - M - J - I - H), I can choose node **J** (or M or I, but J is a good bottleneck).
*   From Path 3 (O - N - B - E - F - X - H), I can choose node **E** (or N, B, F, X, but E is a good bottleneck as it leads to F, which then leads to X and G).

Let's verify if removing the set **[C, J, E]** disconnects O and H:

1.  **Removing C:** This directly breaks the path O - C - H.

2.  **Removing J:** This breaks all paths that go through J to reach I (a neighbor of H).
    *   O - P - D - J - I - H is broken.
    *   O - M - J - I - H is broken.

        *   O - Y - N - B - E - F - X - H is broken.

Based on the number of distinct "entry points" to N's neighborhood (B, C, O), it's highly unlikely that 1 or 2 nodes would suffice, as there are multiple independent routes. 5.  **Attempt to Find a Single Node Cut (Size 1):**
    *   If I remove any single node (e.g., I, J, M, D, P, O, Y, N, C, B, X, K, G, F, L), there will always be at least one path remaining. For example, removing B cuts all paths through B, but paths through F (e.g., H-X-K-F-E) and L (H-I-J-M-L-E) remain. This indicates that a single node removal is not sufficient.

6.  **Attempt to Find a Two Node Cut (Size 2):**
    *   The three "entry points" to E are B, F, and L. These three nodes are distinct and represent independent "channels" for paths to reach E.
    *   **Channel 1 (via L):** H - I - J - M - L - E
    *   **Channel 2 (via B):** Paths from H to B (e.g., H-C-B-E, H-I-J-D-P-O-N-B-E)
    *   **Channel 3 (via F):** Paths from H to F (e.g., H-X-K-F-E, H-G-F-E)

    *   To disconnect H from E, all three of these channels must be severed.
    *   Let's assume we remove two nodes, N1 and N2.
        *   **Case A: N1 cuts Channel 1.** (e.g., N1 = J, M, I, or L). Let's pick J.
            *   After removing J, Channel 1 (H-I-J-M-L-E) is cut. Also, some paths in Channel 2 (those through I-J-D-P-O) are cut.
            *   However, paths in Channel 2 via C (e.g., H-C-B-E) still exist.
            *   All paths in Channel 3 (via F) still exist.
            *   Now, N2 must cut both the remaining paths in Channel 2 (via C to B) AND all paths in Channel 3 (via X/G to F). These two sets of paths are independent. No single node N2 can cut both sets of paths. For example, if N2=C, paths to F remain. If N2=F, paths to B via C remain.
            *   Therefore, if N1 cuts Channel 1, we need at least two more nodes to cut the remaining two channels, making the total cut set size at least 3.

        *   **Case B: N1 cuts Channel 2.** (e.g., N1 = C, B, N, O, Y, P, D, J, I). Let's pick C.
            *   After removing C, paths in Channel 2 via C are cut. Some paths in Channel 2 via I-J-D-P-O still exist.
            *   Channel 1 (H-I-J-M-L-E) still exists.
            *   Channel 3 (via F) still exists.
            *   Now, N2 must cut Channel 1 AND the remaining paths in Channel 2 AND Channel 3. This is impossible with a single node.

        *   **Case C: N1 cuts Channel 3.** (e.g., N1 = X, K, G, or F). Let's pick F.
            *   After removing F, Channel 3 is cut.
            *   Channel 1 (H-I-J-M-L-E) still exists.
            *   Channel 2 (via B) still exists.
            *   Now, N2 must cut Channel 1 AND Channel 2. These two sets of paths are independent. No single node N2 can cut both sets of paths.
            *   Therefore, if N1 cuts Channel 3, we need at least two more nodes to cut the remaining two channels, making the total cut set size at least 3.

    *   This systematic analysis demonstrates that no two nodes can simultaneously cut all three independent channels of paths from H to E. Therefore, a cut set of size 2 is not possible.

7.  **Find a Three Node Cut (Size 3):**
    *   Since a cut set of size 2 is not possible, the minimum must be 3.
    *   Consider the three direct neighbors of E: B, F, and L.
    *   If we remove nodes B, F, and L:
        *   Removing B disconnects E from all paths that would go through B.
        *   Removing F disconnects E from all paths that would go through F.
        *   Removing L disconnects E from all paths that would go through L.
    *   After removing B, F, and L, E has no remaining connections to any other node in the graph (except H, which is not allowed to be removed). Therefore, E is completely disconnected from H.
    *   This is a valid cut set of size 3.

The minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, F, L]

3.  **Consider removing edges connected to L:** To completely disconnect L from the rest of the graph (and thus from J), both of its incident edges, (M, L) and (E, L), must be removed.
4.  **Verify sufficiency:** If both (M, L) and (E, L) are removed, node L becomes isolated. No path can reach L from J or any other node in the graph. Therefore, J and L are disconnected.
5.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   If only one edge, for example (M, L), is removed, then L is still connected to E via (E, L). Since E is connected to B and F, and B and F are part of a larger network that connects back to J (e.g., J-I-C-B-E-L or J-I-H-X-F-E-L), paths between J and L would still exist.
    *   Similarly, if only (E, L) is removed, then L is still connected to M via (M, L). Since M is directly connected to J via (J, M), the path J-M-L would still exist.
    *   Since removing only one edge is not sufficient to disconnect J and L, at least two edges must be removed.
6.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of edges to remove is 2, and the cut set consists of the two edges directly connected to node L.

    *   Neighbors of O: C, N, P, Y. (Y is an end node and does not connect to H's vicinity. So, paths through Y are not relevant.)
    Therefore, all paths from H to O must pass through E, B, or I, and all paths to O must pass through C, N, or P.

3.  **Explore Paths and Potential Cut Sets:**
    Let's list the distinct paths from H to O:
    *   P1: H - B - N - O
    *   P2: H - B - C - O
    *   P3: H - B - C - N - O
    *   P4: H - I - C - O
    *   P5: H - I - P - O
    *   P6: H - I - J - P - O
    *   P7: H - E - I - C - O
    *   P8: H - E - I - P - O
    *   P9: H - E - M - J - P - O

    *   If I remove E: Paths P7, P8, P9 are broken. Paths P1, P2, P3, P4, P5, P6 remain. (e.g., H-B-N-O is still open).
    *   If I remove B: Paths P1, P2, P3 are broken. Paths P4, P5, P6, P7, P8, P9 remain. (e.g., H-I-C-O is still open).
    *   If I remove I: Paths P4, P5, P6, P7, P8 are broken. Paths P1, P2, P3, P9 remain. (e.g., H-B-N-O is still open).
    *   Similarly, removing any single node (C, N, P, M, J, etc.) will leave other paths open.
    Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

5.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**
    Let's consider combinations of two nodes:
    *   If I remove [E, B]: Paths P1, P2, P3, P7, P8, P9 are broken. Paths P4, P5, P6 (via I) remain. (e.g., H-I-C-O is still open).
    *   If I remove [E, I]: Paths P4, P5, P6, P7, P8, P9 are broken. Paths P1, P2, P3 (via B) remain. (e.g., H-B-N-O is still open).
    *   If I remove [B, I]: Paths P1, P2, P3, P4, P5, P6, P7, P8 are broken. Path P9 (H-E-M-J-P-O) remains. (e.g., H-E-M-J-P-O is still open).
    Since removing any two of {E, B, I} leaves a path through the third, these three nodes form a critical set of entry points from H.

    Let's also consider the exit points to O: {C, N, P}.
    *   If I remove [C, N]: Paths P2, P3, P4, P7 are broken. Paths P1, P5, P6, P8, P9 (via P) remain. (e.g., H-I-P-O is still open).
    *   If I remove [C, P]: Paths P2, P3, P4, P5, P6, P7, P8, P9 are broken. Path P1 (H-B-N-O) remains. (e.g., H-B-N-O is still open).
    *   If I remove [N, P]: Paths P1, P3, P5, P6, P8, P9 are broken. Paths P2, P4, P7 (via C) remain. (e.g., H-B-C-O is still open).
    Since removing any two of {C, N, P} leaves a path through the third, these three nodes form a critical set of exit points to O.

    This suggests that a cut set of size 2 is not possible.

6.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Vertex-Disjoint Paths):**
    To confirm the minimum cut size, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between H and O (excluding H and O themselves).
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N - O
        Intermediate nodes: {B, N}

        Intermediate nodes: {I, C}
    *   **Path 3:** H - E - M - J - P - O
        Intermediate nodes: {E, M, J, P}

    The sets of intermediate nodes for these three paths are {B, N}, {I, C}, and {E, M, J, P}. These sets are mutually disjoint.
    Therefore, there are at least 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and O. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect H and O is at least 3.

7.  **Identify a Cut Set of Size 3:**
    Since we've established that the minimum cut size is at least 3, and we've already identified potential cut sets of size 3:
    *   **Cut Set 1: [E, B, I]**
        If nodes E, B, and I are removed, H is only connected to G. G is connected to F, K, X. None of F, K, X are connected to any node in O's neighborhood (C, N, P, Y). Thus, H is completely disconnected from O.
    *   **Cut Set 2: [C, N, P]**
        If nodes C, N, and P are removed, O is only connected to Y. Y is not connected to any node in H's neighborhood (E, G, B, I). Thus, O is completely disconnected from H.

    Both [E, B, I] and [C, N, P] are valid cut sets of size 3. Since we've proven that a cut set of size 2 is impossible, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [E, B, I] (or [C, N, P])

2. Path 2: L - M - F - G - H (Nodes: M, F, G)

This means that to disconnect L from H, we must remove at least one node from Path 1 and at least one node from Path 2. Therefore, a minimum of 2 nodes must be removed. A single node removal is not sufficient.

All paths from L through M are of the form L -> M -> ... -> H.

    - If M is also removed, L has no outgoing edges to any other node. Thus, L is completely disconnected from H.

    *   Node B is connected to: C, E, H, I, N, O.
    *   Node M is connected to: E, J, L.

    Any path from B to M must eventually pass through one of M's direct neighbors: E, J, or L.

3.  **Consider the "M-side" of the graph:**
    Let's define a set of nodes that are "close" to M and through which all paths to M must pass. This set includes M itself, and its direct neighbors E, J, L.

4.  **Identify edges connecting the "B-side" (rest of the graph) to the "M-side" ({E, J, L, M}):**
    We need to find edges where one endpoint is in {E, J, L} (or M, but we can't remove M) and the other endpoint is not in {E, J, L, M} and is reachable from B.

        *   (D, J): Connects D (B-side) to J (M-side). However, D is not connected to B, so any path from B to D to J is impossible. This edge is not relevant for disconnecting B from M.
    *   **Edges connected to L (excluding M):**
        *   (E, L): Connects E (M-side) to L (M-side). This edge is internal to the M-side and does not cross the boundary from the B-side.

5.  **Formulate the cut set:**
    Based on the analysis in step 4, all paths from B to M must traverse one of these three edges: (B, E), (P, E), or (P, J). If we remove these three edges, B will no longer be able to reach E, J, or L, and consequently, will not be able to reach M.

    *   Removing (B, E) cuts the direct path from B to E.
    *   Removing (P, E) cuts all paths from B that go through P to E.
    *   Removing (P, J) cuts all paths from B that go through P to J.

    Therefore, the set of edges `[(B, E), (P, E), (P, J)]` is a cut set. The size of this cut set is 3.

6.  **Verify minimality:**
    To confirm this is the minimum number of edges, we must show that removing any two of these three edges would still leave a path between B and M.

    *   **If (B, E) and (P, E) are removed:**
        A path still exists via (P, J). For example: B - I - P - J - M.
    *   **If (B, E) and (P, J) are removed:**
        A path still exists via (P, E). For example: B - I - P - E - M.
    *   **If (P, E) and (P, J) are removed:**
        A path still exists via (B, E). For example: B - E - M.

    Since removing any two of these three edges leaves at least one path, all three edges are necessary to disconnect B from M.

7.  **Alternative cut set (direct edges to M):**
    Another valid cut set would be to remove all edges directly connected to M: `[(E, M), (J, M), (L, M)]`. This also has a size of 3. Since we found a cut set of size 3, and proved that 2 edges are not enough, the minimum is 3.

The minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

        Edges: {(H, G), (G, F), (F, P), (P, E)}
        These edges are distinct from Path 1's and Path 2's edges. So, Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are all edge-disjoint.

    Since I have found three edge-disjoint paths between H and E, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and E is at least 3.

5.  **Confirm minimum cut set:**
    As established in step 2, removing all edges directly connected to H (i.e., (H, C), (H, I), (H, G)) will completely isolate H from the rest of the graph, and thus from E. This cut set has a size of 3.
    Since we know the minimum cut must be at least 3, and we found a cut set of size 3, this is indeed the minimum.

    Another valid cut set of size 3 would be to remove the "entry" edges to E from the three disjoint paths: [(B, E), (M, E), (P, E)].

The chosen cut set [(H, C), (H, I), (H, G)] ensures that no path can originate from H, thus disconnecting it from E.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node D is connected to nodes J and P.
    *   Node F is connected to nodes M, E, X, K, G.

2.  **Analyze paths to D:**
    Any path from F to D must necessarily pass through one of D's direct neighbors, J or P, and then use the edge connecting that neighbor to D.
    *   Paths to D via J must use the edge (J, D).
    *   Paths to D via P must use the edge (P, D).

3.  **Consider removing a single edge:**
    *   If I remove only the edge (J, D), there are still paths from F to D that go through P. For example, F-M-J-P-D, F-E-B-N-O-P-D, etc.
    *   If I remove only the edge (P, D), there are still paths from F to D that go through J. For example, F-M-J-D, F-X-J-D, etc.
    *   If I remove any other single edge in the graph (e.g., (M, J), (E, B), etc.), there will always be alternative paths that still connect F to both J and P, and thus to D.
    *   Therefore, it is not possible to disconnect F and D by removing only one edge. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

4.  **Consider removing two edges:**
    *   To completely disconnect D from the rest of the graph, and thus from F, I can remove all edges directly connected to D.
    *   The edges directly connected to D are (J, D) and (P, D).
    *   If both (J, D) and (P, D) are removed, node D becomes isolated. An isolated node cannot be part of any path.
    *   Therefore, removing these two edges ensures that no path exists between F and D.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since 1 edge is not sufficient, and 2 edges are sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2. The cut set consists of the two edges directly connected to node D.

Final Answer:

    Therefore, the edges connected to H are (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).

2.  **Consider the implications of these connections:**
    Any path from B to H must, at some point, traverse one of these three edges to reach H. If all three of these edges are removed, H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from B. This forms a potential cut set of size 3: `[(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]`.

3.  **Verify if this cut set is minimal using Menger's Theorem (finding edge-disjoint paths):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum number of edges, I need to find at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and H. Edge-disjoint paths are paths that do not share any common edges.

    Let's identify three such paths:
    *   **Path 1:** B - H
        Edges: `{(B, H)}`

    *   **Path 2:** B - E - F - G - H
        Edges: `{(B, E), (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}`

    *   **Path 3:** B - C - I - H
        Edges: `{(B, C), (C, I), (I, H)}`

4.  **Check for edge-disjointness:**
    *   The edge `(B, H)` is unique to Path 1.
    *   The edges `(B, E)`, `(E, F)`, `(F, G)`, `(G, H)` are unique to Path 2. Node B is connected to E, N, C, H. Path 1 uses (B,H), Path 3 uses (B,C). So (B,E) is not used by other paths.
    *   The edges `(B, C)`, `(C, I)`, `(I, H)` are unique to Path 3. Node B is connected to E, N, C, H. Path 1 uses (B,H), Path 2 uses (B,E). So (B,C) is not used by other paths.

    Since these three paths do not share any common edges, they are edge-disjoint.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    According to Menger's Theorem, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them. Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3. The cut set `[(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]` successfully disconnects H from B by removing all its direct connections to the rest of the graph.

4.  **Consider potential cut sets:**
    *   **Can we disconnect with 1 node?** No, because H has three distinct neighbors (G, B, I). Removing any single one of them would leave paths through the other two. For example, removing G leaves paths E-B-H and E-I-H.
    *   **Can we disconnect with 2 nodes?** Let's test combinations of two nodes from {G, B, I} or other critical nodes.
        *   If we remove {G, B}: Paths like E-I-H would still exist.
        *   If we remove {G, I}: Paths like E-B-H would still exist.
        *   If we remove {B, I}: Paths like E-F-G-H would still exist.
        *   This indicates that removing any two of G, B, or I is not sufficient.
        *   Let's consider other pairs. For example, if we remove {F, B}:
            *   Path E-F-G-H is broken (due to F).
            *   Path E-B-H is broken (due to B).
            *   However, path E-I-H still exists. So {F, B} is not a cut set.
        *   Similarly, removing any other pair of nodes will leave at least one path intact.

5.  **Identify vertex-disjoint paths:** Let's look for paths that do not share any intermediate nodes.
    *   Path 1: E - F - G - H (Intermediate nodes: F, G)
    *   Path 2: E - B - H (Intermediate node: B)
    *   Path 3: E - I - H (Intermediate node: I)
    These three paths are vertex-disjoint with respect to their intermediate nodes (F, G, B, I are all distinct and not E or H). According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them. Since we found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut set must be at least 3.

6.  **Formulate a cut set of size 3:**
    *   Consider the set {G, B, I}.
    *   If G is removed, all paths from E to H that pass through G (e.g., E-F-G-H) are broken.
    *   If B is removed, all paths from E to H that pass through B (e.g., E-B-H, E-I-C-B-H) are broken.
    *   If I is removed, all paths from E to H that pass through I (e.g., E-I-H, E-L-M-J-I-H) are broken.
    *   Since G, B, and I are the only direct neighbors of H, removing all three of them will ensure that no path from E can reach H.

7.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 3, and a valid cut set is {G, B, I}.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [G, B, I]

    *   If we remove (P, J): This breaks any path E-...-P-J.

    This cut set also has a size of 3.

    It appears that no matter which two edges are chosen, a path between E and J remains. This confirms that a minimum of 3 edges is required.

Since we found a cut set of size 3 and confirmed that no smaller cut set exists, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    *   Node E is connected to F, M, L, and B.

    *   If I remove only the edge (P, D), there are still paths from E to D via J. For example, E-M-J-D.

    *   If D is isolated, no path can exist from E to D.
    *   This cut set, [(J, D), (P, D)], consists of 2 edges.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    As established in step 3, one edge is not enough. Since we found a set of 2 edges that disconnects E and D, this set represents the minimum number of edges required.

6.  **Confirm all paths are broken:**
    By removing (J, D) and (P, D), node D becomes an isolated component. No other node can reach D, thus no path exists from E to D.

Step-by-step reasoning. The two black nodes are labeled I and G. I will perform a breadth-first search (BFS) starting from node I to find the shortest path to node G.

1.  **Initialize:**
    *   Queue: `[(I, [I])]` (Node, Path)
    *   Visited: `{}`

2.  **Level 0:**
    *   Current node: I
    *   Path: `[I]`
    *   Add I to Visited: `{I}`

3.  **Level 1 (Neighbors of I):**
    *   Pop `(I, [I])` from queue.
    *   Neighbors of I are P, C, H.
    *   Add to queue:
        *   `(P, [I, P])`
        *   `(C, [I, C])`
        *   `(H, [I, H])`
    *   Add P, C, H to Visited: `{I, P, C, H}`
    *   Current queue: `[(P, [I, P]), (C, [I, C]), (H, [I, H])]`
    *   None of these neighbors are the target node G.

4.  **Level 2 (Neighbors of Level 1 nodes):**
    *   Pop `(P, [I, P])` from queue.
        *   Neighbors of P: O, J, D (I is already visited).
        *   Add to queue: `(O, [I, P, O])`, `(J, [I, P, J])`, `(D, [I, P, D])`.
        *   Add O, J, D to Visited: `{I, P, C, H, O, J, D}`
    *   Pop `(C, [I, C])` from queue.
        *   Neighbors of C: N, O, B (I, O are already visited).
        *   Add to queue: `(N, [I, C, N])`, `(B, [I, C, B])`.
        *   Add N, B to Visited: `{I, P, C, H, O, J, D, N, B}`
    *   Pop `(H, [I, H])` from queue.
        *   Neighbors of H: B, G (I, B are already visited).
        *   One neighbor is **G**, which is the target node!
        *   The path found is `[I, H, G]`.

Since the target node G was found at Level 2, the shortest path has a length of 2.

**Verification:**
*   Is I directly connected to H? Yes, there is a continuous black line.
*   Is H directly connected to G? Yes, there is a continuous black line.
*   The path `[I, H, G]` is valid and has a length of 2.
*   Since BFS guarantees the first path found to the target is the shortest, no path of length 1 exists.

Final Answer: Length: 2, Path: [I, H, G]

    *   Node G is connected to: X, K, F, E, H.
    *   Node N is connected to: B, C, O, Y.

2.  **Analyze the graph structure to find potential bottlenecks:**
    *   All paths originating from G must pass through one of its direct neighbors: X, K, F, E, H.
    *   Nodes X, K, F form a small cluster with G. F is also connected to E.
    *   Nodes E and H appear to be the primary gateways from the G-side of the graph to the rest of the network that eventually leads to N.

3.  **Consider isolating G from the rest of the graph:**
    *   To disconnect G from the entire graph (and thus from N), we need to remove all edges connecting G's component to the rest of the graph.
    *   The nodes directly connected to G are X, K, F, E, H.
    *   The edges (G,X), (G,K), (G,F), (G,E), (G,H) are direct connections from G.
    *   However, F is also connected to E. This means even if (G,E) is cut, a path G-F-E still exists to reach E.
    *   Therefore, to completely isolate the component {G, X, K, F} from the rest of the graph, we must cut all edges that lead out of this component. These edges are:
        *   (G,E)
        *   (G,H)
        *   (F,E) (since F is connected to G, and E leads to N)

4.  **Evaluate this potential cut set:**

    *   Number of edges: 3.
    *   After removing these three edges, G is only connected to X, K, and F. The nodes X, K, F are only connected to G, X, K, F. None of these nodes (G, X, K, F) have any remaining connections to the rest of the graph.
    *   Therefore, G is completely disconnected from the rest of the graph, including N. This is a valid cut set.

5.  **Consider alternative cut sets and verify minimality:**
    *   Could we disconnect G and N with fewer than 3 edges?
    *   Let's assume we remove only two edges.
    *   If we remove any two edges from the set {(G,E), (G,H), (F,E)}, one edge will always remain, allowing a path from G to E or H, and thus to N.
        *   If (G,E) and (G,H) are removed, G-F-E still connects G to E.
        *   If (G,E) and (F,E) are removed, G-H still connects G to H.
        *   If (G,H) and (F,E) are removed, G-E still connects G to E.
    *   Since all paths from G to N must eventually pass through E or H (or nodes reachable from E or H), and we've shown that isolating G from E and H requires at least 3 edges, a cut of size 2 is not possible.

6.  **Confirm the minimum number of edges:**
    *   Since a cut of size 2 is not possible, and we found a valid cut of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Image name: FD-FR_task2_gt3_PAIR1
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 5
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are N and P. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect N and P. I cannot remove N or P.

2.  **Explore paths between N and P:** I will systematically identify distinct paths from N to P. To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for node-disjoint paths. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes in a vertex cut (excluding the source and sink) is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between the source and sink.

    *   **Path 1:** N - O - P
        *   Intermediate node: {O}

    *   **Path 2:** N - C - I - P
        *   Intermediate nodes: {C, I}
        *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 1 (O is not C or I).

    *   **Path 3:** N - B - H - G - F - E - M - J - P
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B, H, G, F, E, M, J}
        *   Let's verify if this path is node-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.
            *   It does not use O (from Path 1).
            *   It does not use C or I (from Path 2).
        *   Therefore, this path is node-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

A common strategy is to partition the graph into two sets, one containing J (let's call it the "J-side") and one containing F (the "F-side"), and then identify all edges that cross this partition.

3.  **Define the "F-side" cluster:** Let's consider the set of nodes directly connected to F, along with F itself, as the "F-side" cluster: S_F = {F, E, K, X, G}. Any path from J to F must eventually enter this cluster.

4.  **Identify edges connecting to the "F-side" cluster from the rest of the graph:** We need to find all edges (U, V) where U is not in S_F and V is in S_F.

    *   (L, E)
    *   (H, X)
    *   (H, G)

        *   It does not use X, K, J, P (from Path 2).

- All paths from M to B that go through H, C, or N (which are the other direct neighbors of B) must first pass through the edge (G, H) to reach H, and then subsequently I, C, or N. Therefore, removing the edge (G, H) will cut all these paths.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are H and N. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect H and N. I cannot remove H or N.

2.  **Explore Paths:** I will systematically identify paths from H to N. To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove (vertex cut), I can use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of vertices whose removal disconnects two non-adjacent vertices is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

3.  **Find Vertex-Disjoint Paths:** I will search for paths from H to N that do not share any intermediate nodes.

    *   **Path 1:** A direct path from H to N is:
        H - B - N
        (Intermediate node: B)

    *   **Path 2:** Now, I look for a path from H to N that does not use node B.
        H - E - M - J - P - O - N
        (Intermediate nodes: E, M, J, P, O. None of these are B.)
        So, Path 1 and Path 2 are vertex-disjoint.

    *   **Path 3:** Next, I look for a path from H to N that does not use any intermediate nodes from Path 1 (B) or Path 2 (E, M, J, P, O).
        From H, I can go to I.
        H - I.
        From I, I need to reach N without using B, E, M, J, P, O.
        I is connected to C, P, J, E. P, J, E are already used in Path 2. So, I must go to C.
        H - I - C.

        C is connected to N, O, B, I. N is the target. O and B are used in Path 1 or Path 2.
        So, the path is: H - I - C - N
        (Intermediate nodes: I, C. None of these are B, E, M, J, P, O.)
        So, Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are all vertex-disjoint.

    *   **Summary of Vertex-Disjoint Paths:**
        1.  H - B - N (Intermediate node: B)
        2.  H - E - M - J - P - O - N (Intermediate nodes: E, M, J, P, O)
        3.  H - I - C - N (Intermediate nodes: I, C)

4.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** Since I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and N, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect H and N is at least 3.

5.  **Identify a Cut Set of Size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects H and N. A straightforward approach is to take one intermediate node from each of the vertex-disjoint paths found.
    *   From Path 1: B
    *   From Path 2: E (or M, J, P, O)
    *   From Path 3: I (or C)

    Let's choose the set {B, E, I}.
    *   Removing B breaks H - B - N.
    *   Removing E breaks H - E - M - J - P - O - N (and any other path starting H-E...).
    *   Removing I breaks H - I - C - N (and any other path starting H-I...).

    All paths from H to N must pass through B, E, or I (considering H-G-F-E is covered by H-E). Therefore, removing {B, E, I} will disconnect H from N.

6.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 3, and one such cut set is {B, E, I}.

The final answer is:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, E, I]

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 5
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are M and B. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect M from B.

1.  **Identify all direct connections from M:**
    *   M is connected to J.
    *   M is connected to O.
    *   M is connected to L.
    These are the only three edges originating from M.

2.  **Consider a simple cut set:**
    If we remove all edges directly connected to M, M will be isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from B.
    The edges are (M, J), (M, O), and (M, L).
    This cut set has a size of 3. This is a candidate for the minimum.

3.  **Attempt to find a smaller cut set (size 1 or 2):**
    *   **Can we disconnect M and B by removing 1 edge?**
        No, because M has three distinct outgoing paths (via J, O, L) and B has four distinct incoming paths (via O, N, C, E). Removing any single edge would leave multiple alternative paths. For example, removing (M, O) would still leave paths M-J-...-B and M-L-...-B.
    *   **Can we disconnect M and B by removing 2 edges?**
        Let's consider the main "branches" of paths from M to B:
        *   Branch 1 (via O): M - O - B (and M - O - N - B)
        *   Branch 2 (via J): M - J - ... - B (e.g., M - J - I - C - B)
        *   Branch 3 (via L): M - L - ... - B (e.g., M - L - E - B)

        Let's try removing any two edges directly connected to M:
        *   If (M, J) and (M, O) are removed: Path M - L - E - B still exists.
        *   If (M, J) and (M, L) are removed: Path M - O - B still exists.
        *   If (M, O) and (M, L) are removed: Path M - J - I - C - B still exists.
        Therefore, removing any two edges directly connected to M is not sufficient.

        Let's try removing any two edges directly connected to B:
        *   If (B, O) and (B, N) are removed: Paths M - J - I - C - B and M - L - E - B still exist.
        *   If (B, O) and (B, C) are removed: Paths M - O - N - B and M - L - E - B still exist.
        *   If (B, O) and (B, E) are removed: Paths M - O - N - B and M - J - I - C - B still exist.
        *   If (B, N) and (B, C) are removed: Paths M - O - B and M - L - E - B still exist.
        *   If (B, N) and (B, E) are removed: Paths M - O - B and M - J - I - C - B still exist.
        *   If (B, C) and (B, E) are removed: Paths M - O - B and M - O - N - B still exist.
        Therefore, removing any two edges directly connected to B is not sufficient.

        Let's try removing one edge from M's direct connections and one from B's direct connections, or two internal edges. This becomes complex to enumerate exhaustively, but we can use Menger's theorem.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Edge Version):**
    Menger's theorem states that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges needed to disconnect them.
    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from M to B:
    *   **Path 1:** M - O - B. Edges: {(M, O), (O, B)}
    *   **Path 2:** M - J - I - C - B. Edges: {(M, J), (J, I), (I, C), (C, B)}
    *   **Path 3:** M - L - E - B. Edges: {(M, L), (L, E), (E, B)}

    Let's verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   The edges {(M, O), (O, B)} are unique to Path 1.
    *   The edges {(M, J), (J, I), (I, C), (C, B)} are unique to Path 2.
    *   The edges {(M, L), (L, E), (E, B)} are unique to Path 3.
    Indeed, these three paths share no common edges.

    Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and B, according to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect M from B must be at least 3.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    We found a cut set of size 3: [(M, J), (M, O), (M, L)].
    We also proved that the minimum cut must be at least 3 by finding 3 edge-disjoint paths.
    Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

        *   This cut set successfully disconnects M from N.

    *   **Option 2: Consider a cut between the "left" and "right" parts of the graph.**
        Let's define the "M-side" as the set of nodes {M, L, E, G, F, K, X} and the "N-side" as the set of nodes {N, B, C, O, Y, J, D, P, I, H}.
        The edges that connect the M-side to the N-side are:
        *   (M, J)
        *   (E, H)
        If we remove these two edges:
        *   (M, J) breaks all direct paths from M to J and subsequently to the N-side via J.
        *   (E, H) breaks all paths from E to H and subsequently to the N-side via H.
        *   After removing (M, J) and (E, H), M is connected to L and E. E is connected to M, L, G. G is connected to E, F, H, K, X. H is connected to G, B, I.
        *   However, the edge (E, H) is removed, so E cannot reach H.
        *   The edge (M, J) is removed, so M cannot reach J.
        *   Therefore, M can only reach nodes {L, E, G, F, K, X}. None of these nodes are connected to the N-side anymore.
        *   This cut set also successfully disconnects M from N.

6.  **Conclusion:** Both [(M, E), (M, J)] and [(M, J), (E, H)] are valid cut sets of size 2. Since a cut of size 1 is not possible, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(M, E), (M, J)]

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are N and H. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect N from H.

1.  **Identify all direct and indirect paths between N and H.**
    Let's start by listing the neighbors of N and H:
    *   Neighbors of N: Y, O, C, B
    *   Neighbors of H: G, I, C, B

2.  **Look for short, distinct paths:**
    *   **Path 1:** N - C - H (Edges: (N, C), (C, H))
    *   **Path 2:** N - B - H (Edges: (N, B), (B, H))
    *   **Path 3:** N - O - P - I - H (Edges: (N, O), (O, P), (P, I), (I, H))

3.  **Check if these paths are edge-disjoint:**
    *   Path 1 uses edges (N, C) and (C, H).
    *   Path 2 uses edges (N, B) and (B, H).
    *   Path 3 uses edges (N, O), (O, P), (P, I), and (I, H).
    These three paths do not share any common edges. Therefore, they are edge-disjoint.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:**
    Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between N and H, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 3.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    To achieve disconnection with 3 edges, we need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal breaks all paths. A straightforward approach is to remove all edges connecting one of the target nodes to the rest of the graph, except for any edges that lead to nodes that are only connected to that target node (like Y for N).
    *   Node N is connected to Y, O, C, and B.
    *   Node Y is only connected to N. Therefore, the edge (N, Y) does not lead to H or any other path to H.
    *   The edges (N, O), (N, C), and (N, B) are the only edges connecting N to the rest of the graph that could potentially lead to H.

    Let's propose the cut set: `[(N, O), (N, C), (N, B)]`.

6.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    If we remove the edges (N, O), (N, C), and (N, B):
    *   Node N is no longer connected to O, C, or B.
    *   Node N remains connected only to Y.
    *   Since Y is exclusively connected to N, N becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph.
    *   Consequently, no path can exist from N to H.

This cut set of 3 edges successfully disconnects N from H, and we have established that a minimum of 3 edges is required.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(N, O), (N, C), (N, B)]

    *   Node H is connected to: B, I, G.
    *   Node N is connected to: B, C, Y, O.

2.  **Consider a simple approach: isolating one of the target nodes.**
    *   To isolate H, I would need to remove all edges connected to it: (H, B), (H, I), and (H, G). This is a cut set of 3 edges.
    *   To isolate N, I would need to remove all edges connected to it: (N, B), (N, C), (N, Y), and (N, O). This is a cut set of 4 edges, which is not minimal compared to 3.
    *   Therefore, a cut set of 3 edges is a candidate for the minimum.

3.  **Explore the possibility of a smaller cut (e.g., 1 or 2 edges).**
    *   A single edge removal cannot disconnect H from N, as both H and N have multiple connections. For example, if (H,B) is removed, paths like H-I-C-N or H-G-E-B-N still exist.
    *   To determine if a cut of 2 edges is possible, I need to check if all paths between H and N can be broken by removing just two edges. This is often equivalent to finding if there are at most two edge-disjoint paths between H and N (by Menger's Theorem).

4.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths between H and N:**
    I will search for paths from H to N that do not share any common edges.

    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        *   Edges: {(H, B), (B, N)}

    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
        *   Edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, N)}
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as it uses different edges.

    *   **Path 3:** H - G - E - B - O - N
        *   Edges: {(H, G), (G, E), (E, B), (B, O), (O, N)}
        *   Let's check if this path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2:
            *   It does not use (H, B) or (B, N) from Path 1.
            *   It does not use (H, I), (I, C), or (C, N) from Path 2.
        *   Therefore, this path is edge-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.

5.  **Conclusion based on edge-disjoint paths:**
    I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and N is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size must be at least 3.

6.  **Verify the candidate cut set of 3 edges:**
    The cut set [(H, B), (H, I), (H, G)] consists of the three edges directly connected to H.
    *   Removing (H, B) breaks all paths starting with H-B.
    *   Removing (H, I) breaks all paths starting with H-I.
    *   Removing (H, G) breaks all paths starting with H-G.
    Since all paths from H to N must start with one of these three edges, removing them will completely disconnect H from N.

7.  **Final determination:**

    *   G - X - K - J - P - O

*   If (N,B) and (N,C) are removed, paths like G-F-K-J-P-O-N would still exist.
*   If (N,B) and (N,O) are removed, paths like G-H-C-N would still exist.
*   If (N,C) and (N,O) are removed, paths like G-H-B-N would still exist.

According to Menger's Theorem, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them. If we can find 4 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size is at least 4.

    Let's identify four edge-disjoint paths from F to D:

   *   H - G - X - F - E - L - M - J - P - O

**Attempting a cut set of size 1:**
*   If I remove I: Paths through B and G still exist (e.g., H-B-C-O, H-G-F-K-J-P-O).
*   If I remove B: Paths through I and G still exist (e.g., H-I-O, H-G-F-K-J-P-O).
*   If I remove G: Paths through I and B still exist (e.g., H-I-O, H-B-C-O).
*   If I remove C, N, Y, P, J, F, K, X, E, L, M, D: In each case, multiple paths would remain.
Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient. The minimum cut set size is greater than 1.

**Attempting a cut set of size 2:**
Based on the three distinct "branches" from H (through I, B, and G), it's unlikely that two nodes can cut all three. For example, if I remove I and B, paths through G (e.g., H-G-F-K-J-P-O) still exist.

**Finding vertex-disjoint paths (Menger's Theorem):**
To confirm the minimum number of nodes, I will identify vertex-disjoint paths between H and O (excluding H and O themselves).
1.  **Path 1:** H - I - O (Uses node I)
2.  **Path 2:** H - B - C - O (Uses nodes B, C. These are distinct from I.)
3.  **Path 3:** H - G - F - K - J - P - O (Uses nodes G, F, K, J, P. These are distinct from I, B, C.)

I have found three vertex-disjoint paths between H and O. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect H and O (a minimum vertex cut) must be at least 3.

**Constructing a cut set of size 3:**
To break all paths, I need to cut each of the three main branches identified:
*   To cut paths through I: Remove node **I**.
*   To cut paths through B: Remove node **B**.
*   To cut paths through G: All paths from G to O eventually pass through J and then P. Therefore, removing either **J** or **P** will sever all paths originating from G.

So, a valid cut set of size 3 would be [I, B, J] or [I, B, P].

Let's verify [I, B, J]:
*   Removing I breaks H-I-O and H-I-P-O.
*   Removing B breaks H-B-C-O and H-B-N-O.
*   Removing J breaks all paths from H-G-... to O (e.g., H-G-F-K-J-P-O, H-G-F-E-M-J-P-O, etc., as J is a bottleneck for all paths from G to P and then O).

All paths are disconnected. Since I found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, and a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are E and I. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and I.
2.  **Initial Assessment (Single Node Cut):** I first check if a single node can disconnect E and I.
    *   Neighbors of E: L, M, F, G, B.
    *   Neighbors of I: P, H, C, O.
    *   Removing any single node from the graph (e.g., J, H, B, C, P, O, G, M, F, L, X, K, N, Y, D) will always leave at least one path between E and I. For example, removing G still leaves paths through B, M, and F. Removing J still leaves paths through G, F, and B. Therefore, a single node is not sufficient. The minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.
3.  **Identify Node-Disjoint Paths (Menger's Theorem):** To find the minimum cut set, I look for node-disjoint paths between E and I. If I can find *k* node-disjoint paths, then at least *k* nodes must be removed to disconnect E and I.
    *   **Path 1 (P1):** E - G - H - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {G, H}
    *   **Path 2 (P2):** E - B - C - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B, C}
    *   **Path 3 (P3):** E - M - J - P - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J, P}
    *   Let's verify if these paths are node-disjoint (excluding E and I):
        *   P1 intermediate nodes: {G, H}
        *   P2 intermediate nodes: {B, C}
        *   P3 intermediate nodes: {M, J, P}
        *   All sets of intermediate nodes are distinct. Thus, these three paths are node-disjoint.
    *   Since there are 3 node-disjoint paths, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect E and I.
4.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects E and I. Based on the node-disjoint paths identified, a natural candidate for a cut set would be to pick one intermediate node from each path.
    *   From P1 (E-G-H-I), I can pick H.
    *   From P2 (E-B-C-I), I can pick B.
    *   From P3 (E-M-J-P-I), I can pick J.
    *   Let's test the cut set: **[J, H, B]**.
5.  **Verify the Cut Set [J, H, B]:**
    *   **Paths through J:** All paths that go through J (e.g., E-L-M-J-P-I, E-M-J-O-C-I) are broken because J is removed.
    *   **Paths through H:** All paths that go through H (e.g., E-G-H-I, E-F-G-H-I, E-F-K-G-H-I, E-F-X-K-G-H-I) are broken because H is removed.
    *   **Paths through B:** All paths that go through B (e.g., E-B-C-I, E-B-N-C-I, E-B-N-Y-O-C-I, E-B-N-Y-O-P-I, E-B-C-O-P-I) are broken because B is removed.
    *   Since all identified major branches and their sub-paths are severed by removing J, H, and B, this set successfully disconnects E and I.
6.  **Conclusion:** I have found 3 node-disjoint paths, which means at least 3 nodes must be removed. I have also found a cut set of size 3 ([J, H, B]) that successfully disconnects E and I. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are L and J.
2.  **Analyze Connections from Node L:** Node L is connected to only two other nodes: E and M.
    *   Edge 1: (L, E)
    *   Edge 2: (L, M)
3.  **Identify Initial Paths:** All paths originating from L towards J must necessarily start by traversing either the edge (L, E) or the edge (L, M).
    *   Example Path 1: L - M - J
    *   Example Path 2: L - E - B - C - O - J (and many others starting with L-E)
4.  **Evaluate Single Edge Removal:**
    *   If we remove only (L, E), then L can still reach J via the path L - M - J.
    *   If we remove only (L, M), then L can still reach J via any path starting with L - E (e.g., L - E - B - C - O - J).
    *   Therefore, removing a single edge is not sufficient to disconnect L from J. This implies that the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.
5.  **Evaluate Two Edge Removal:**
    *   Consider removing both edges directly connected to L: (L, E) and (L, M).
    *   If (L, E) is removed, no path can begin with L-E.
    *   If (L, M) is removed, no path can begin with L-M.
    *   With both edges removed, node L becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between L and J.
6.  **Conclusion:** Since we have established that at least 2 edges must be removed, and we have found a set of 2 edges whose removal disconnects L from J, this set represents the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(L, E), (L, M)]

    *   Neighbors of L: M, E, F
    *   Neighbors of J: D, P, I, M

3.  **Trace All Possible Paths:** I will systematically list paths from L to J.

        *   Path 2: L -> M -> P -> J
    *   **Paths starting L -> E:**
        *   Path 3: L -> E -> B -> N -> Y -> O -> P -> J

        *   Path 6: L -> E -> B -> H -> I -> J

        *   Path 8: L -> F -> K -> G -> H -> I -> J

    *   Removing M: Paths L->E...J and L->F...J still exist. (e.g., L->E->B->C->I->J)
    *   Removing E: Paths L->M->J and L->F...J still exist. (e.g., L->M->J)
    *   Removing F: Paths L->M->J and L->E...J still exist. (e.g., L->M->J)
    *   Removing P: Paths L->M->J and L->E->B->C->I->J and L->F...I->J still exist. (e.g., L->M->J)
    *   Removing I: Paths L->M->J and L->M->P->J and L->E->B->N->Y->O->P->J still exist. (e.g., L->M->J)
    *   No single node can disconnect L and J.

5.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**
    *   Consider removing two nodes from {M, E, F}:
        *   [M, E]: Path L -> F -> K -> G -> H -> I -> J still exists.
        *   [M, F]: Path L -> E -> B -> C -> I -> J still exists.
        *   [E, F]: Path L -> M -> J still exists.
        *   So, removing two nodes from L's immediate neighbors is not enough.
    *   Consider removing two nodes from {P, I} (the main gateways to J, excluding D which is a leaf):
        *   [P, I]: Path L -> M -> J still exists.
        *   So, removing P and I is not enough.
    *   Consider removing one node from {M, E, F} and one from {P, I}:
        *   [M, P]: Paths L -> E -> B -> C -> I -> J and L -> F -> K -> G -> H -> I -> J still exist (they use I).
        *   [M, I]: Path L -> E -> B -> N -> Y -> O -> P -> J still exists (it uses P).
        *   [E, P]: Paths L -> M -> J and L -> F -> K -> G -> H -> I -> J still exist.
        *   [E, I]: Paths L -> M -> J and L -> M -> P -> J and L -> F -> K -> G -> H -> I -> J still exist.
        *   [F, P]: Paths L -> M -> J and L -> E -> B -> C -> I -> J still exist.
        *   [F, I]: Paths L -> M -> J and L -> M -> P -> J and L -> E -> B -> N -> Y -> O -> P -> J still exist.
    *   No combination of two nodes is sufficient to disconnect L and J.

6.  **Identify Vertex-Disjoint Paths (Menger's Theorem):** To confirm the minimum number of nodes, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between L and J (excluding L and J themselves).
    *   Path A: L - M - J (Nodes: {M})
    *   Path B: L - E - B - N - Y - O - P - J (Nodes: {E, B, N, Y, O, P})
    *   Path C: L - F - K - G - H - I - J (Nodes: {F, K, G, H, I})
    These three paths are vertex-disjoint. This means that at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect L and J.

7.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:**

    *   **Remove M:** This breaks Path 1 (L -> M -> J) and Path 2 (L -> M -> P -> J).
    *   **After removing M:** All remaining paths from L to J must go through E or F from L, and then eventually reach J via P or I (since D is a leaf node for J and M is removed).
        *   Paths starting L -> E:
            *   L -> E -> B -> N -> Y -> O -> P -> J (This path uses P)
            *   L -> E -> B -> N -> C -> I -> J (This path uses I)
            *   L -> E -> B -> C -> I -> J (This path uses I)
            *   L -> E -> B -> H -> I -> J (This path uses I)
        *   Paths starting L -> F:
            *   L -> F -> X -> K -> G -> H -> I -> J (This path uses I)

Node D is a leaf node connected only to P and J, so it's not a critical "entry point" for paths from E. Therefore, all paths from E to J must pass through one of the nodes M, I, or P.

Let's analyze if removing a single node can disconnect E and J:
*   If I remove M: Paths E-F-...-I-J and E-B-...-P-J would still exist.
*   If I remove I: Path E-M-J and paths E-B-...-P-J would still exist.
*   If I remove P: Path E-M-J and paths E-F-...-I-J and E-B-...-I-J would still exist.
Therefore, removing a single node is not sufficient.

Let's analyze if removing two nodes can disconnect E and J:
*   If I remove M and I: Path E-B-C-N-O-P-J and E-B-O-P-J would still exist (passing through P).
*   If I remove M and P: Paths E-F-G-H-I-J, E-F-K-X-G-H-I-J, E-B-H-I-J, and E-B-C-I-J would still exist (passing through I).
*   If I remove I and P: Path E-M-J would still exist (passing through M).
Therefore, removing any two of the nodes {M, I, P} is not sufficient to disconnect E and J.

Since all paths from E to J must pass through at least one of M, I, or P, to completely disconnect E from J, I must remove all three of these nodes.

Let's verify that removing M, I, and P disconnects E and J:
*   Path 1 (E - M - J) is broken by removing M.
*   Paths 2, 3, 4, 5 (all ending in ... - I - J) are broken by removing I.
*   Paths 6, 7 (all ending in ... - P - J) are broken by removing P.
All paths are successfully broken.

Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 5
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect the two black nodes, B and M. I will use Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

1.  **Identify all edges in the graph:**
    (K,X), (K,G), (K,F)
    (X,G), (X,F)
    (G,F), (G,H)
    (F,E)
    (H,B), (H,I)
    (B,C), (B,E)
    (E,L), (E,M), (E,I)
    (L,M)
    (M,J)
    (I,C), (I,P), (I,J)
    (C,N), (C,O)
    (N,Y), (N,O)
    (Y,O)
    (O,P)
    (P,J), (P,D)
    (J,D)

2.  **Identify the target nodes:** B and M.

3.  **Find edge-disjoint paths between B and M:**
    I will systematically search for paths that do not share any common edges.

    *   **Path 1 (P1): B - E - M**
        Edges: {(B,E), (E,M)}

    *   **Path 2 (P2): B - H - I - J - M**
        Edges: {(B,H), (H,I), (I,J), (J,M)}
        Let's check if P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint.
        Edges in P1: {(B,E), (E,M)}
        Edges in P2: {(B,H), (H,I), (I,J), (J,M)}
        There are no common edges. So, P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint.

    *   **Path 3 (P3):** Now, I need to find a third path from B to M that is edge-disjoint from both P1 and P2.
        Edges used by P1 and P2: S_edges = {(B,E), (E,M), (B,H), (H,I), (I,J), (J,M)}

        Let's try to construct a path using available edges:
        Start from B, (B,C) is available (not in S_edges).
        From C, (C,I) is available (not in S_edges).
        From I, (I,E) is available (not in S_edges). (Note: (I,J) is in S_edges, (I,P) is available).
        From E, (E,L) is available (not in S_edges). (Note: (E,M) is in S_edges).
        From L, (L,M) is available (not in S_edges).

        So, Path 3 (P3): B - C - I - E - L - M
        Edges: {(B,C), (C,I), (I,E), (E,L), (L,M)}

        Let's verify if P3 is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2:
        P1 edges: {(B,E), (E,M)}
        P2 edges: {(B,H), (H,I), (I,J), (J,M)}
        P3 edges: {(B,C), (C,I), (I,E), (E,L), (L,M)}
        There are no common edges between any pair of these three paths. Therefore, P1, P2, and P3 are edge-disjoint.

4.  **Determine the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths:**
    I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths. This means the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths is at least 3.
    To confirm it's exactly 3, I would need to show that no fourth edge-disjoint path exists. However, for the purpose of finding the minimum cut, finding 3 edge-disjoint paths is sufficient to establish that the minimum cut is at least 3.

5.  **Determine the minimum edge cut:**
    By Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect B and M is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths. Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

6.  **Identify a cut set of 3 edges:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect M is to remove all edges directly connected to it.
    Edges connected to M are: (E,M), (L,M), (J,M).
    If these three edges are removed, M becomes isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from B. This is a valid cut set of size 3.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are I and E. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect I from E, without removing I or E themselves.

First, I identify all possible paths from I to E. I can categorize these paths by the first node they visit from I, or by the last node they visit before E.

Let's list the direct neighbors of I: H, C, P, J.
Let's list the direct neighbors of E: P, L, M, B.

Notice that node P is a common neighbor to both I and E.
Also, node L is only connected to E and M. Any path to E via L must also pass through M (if coming from I's side). So, the effective "entry points" to E from the rest of the graph are P, M, and B. This means any path from I to E *must* pass through at least one of these three nodes: P, M, or B.

Let's test if removing a single node can disconnect I and E:
- If P is removed: Paths like I-P-E are broken. However, paths like I-J-M-E, I-C-B-E, I-C-O-B-E, I-C-N-O-B-E would still exist. So, P alone is not enough.
- If M is removed: Paths like I-J-M-E are broken. However, paths like I-P-E, I-C-B-E, I-C-O-B-E, I-C-N-O-B-E, I-H-G-F-P-E, I-J-P-E would still exist. So, M alone is not enough.
- If B is removed: Paths like I-C-B-E, I-C-O-B-E, I-C-N-O-B-E are broken. However, paths like I-P-E, I-J-M-E, I-H-G-F-P-E, I-J-P-E, I-C-O-P-E, I-C-N-O-P-E would still exist. So, B alone is not enough.
Since any path from I to E must pass through P, M, or B, and removing any single one of them leaves paths through the others, a single node removal is not sufficient.

Next, let's test if removing two nodes can disconnect I and E. We need to consider combinations of P, M, and B, as these are the critical "bottleneck" nodes for reaching E.
- If P and M are removed:
    - Paths through P (e.g., I-P-E, I-J-P-E, I-C-O-P-E, I-H-G-F-P-E) are broken.
    - Paths through M (e.g., I-J-M-E) are broken.
    - However, paths through B still exist, for example: I-C-B-E, I-C-O-B-E, I-C-N-O-B-E. Thus, [P, M] is not a cut set.
- If P and B are removed:
    - Paths through P are broken.
    - Paths through B (e.g., I-C-B-E, I-C-O-B-E, I-C-N-O-B-E) are broken.
    - However, paths through M still exist, for example: I-J-M-E. Thus, [P, B] is not a cut set.
- If M and B are removed:
    - Paths through M are broken.
    - Paths through B are broken.
    - However, paths through P still exist, for example: I-P-E, I-J-P-E, I-C-O-P-E, I-H-G-F-P-E. Thus, [M, B] is not a cut set.

Since removing any two of the three critical nodes (P, M, B) still leaves at least one path between I and E, a cut set of size 2 is not possible.

Finally, let's consider removing all three nodes: P, M, and B.
- If P, M, and B are all removed:
    - All paths that go through P (e.g., I-P-E, I-J-P-E, I-C-O-P-E, I-H-G-F-P-E) are broken.
    - All paths that go through M (e.g., I-J-M-E) are broken.
    - All paths that go through B (e.g., I-C-B-E, I-C-O-B-E, I-C-N-O-B-E) are broken.
Since P, M, and B are the only nodes directly connected to E (excluding L, which is only connected to E and M, and thus covered by M), removing these three nodes will completely disconnect E from the rest of the graph, and therefore from I.

Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [P, M, B]

    *   Visited: `{K}`

    *   Neighbors of E: L, M, F, G, B
    *   Neighbors of H: G, I, B

2.  **Explore paths between E and H:**
    I will look for edge-disjoint paths between E and H. According to Menger's Theorem, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges needed to disconnect them.

    Let's identify some distinct paths:
    *   **Path 1 (P1): E - G - H**
        Edges: {(E, G), (G, H)}
    *   **Path 2 (P2): E - B - H**
        Edges: {(E, B), (B, H)}

    These two paths (P1 and P2) are edge-disjoint. This means we need to remove at least 2 edges to disconnect E and H.

    Let's look for a third edge-disjoint path.
    *   **Path 3 (P3): E - M - J - P - I - H**
        Edges: {(E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, H)}

3.  **Verify edge-disjointness of P1, P2, and P3:**
    *   P1 edges: (E, G), (G, H)
    *   P2 edges: (E, B), (B, H)
    *   P3 edges: (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, H)

    Comparing the edge sets, all edges in P1, P2, and P3 are distinct. For example:
    *   (E, G) is only in P1.
    *   (G, H) is only in P1.
    *   (E, B) is only in P2.
    *   (B, H) is only in P2.
    *   (E, M) is only in P3.
    *   (M, J) is only in P3.
    *   (J, P) is only in P3.
    *   (P, I) is only in P3.
    *   (I, H) is only in P3.

    Since I have found three edge-disjoint paths between E and H, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect H from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to H.
    The edges connected to H are: (G, H), (B, H), and (I, H).
    If these three edges are removed, H becomes an isolated node, and no path can exist between E and H.

    This cut set, [(G, H), (B, H), (I, H)], has a size of 3.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we established that at least 3 edges must be removed (due to 3 edge-disjoint paths), and we found a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects E and H, this is the minimum number of edges required.

2.  **Analyze Paths from H:** I will systematically explore all possible paths from H to O. I can categorize the paths based on the first step from H:
    *   **Paths starting with H-G or H-F:**
        *   H is connected to G and F. G is connected to F, K, X. F is connected to G, K, E. K and X are only connected to G and F and do not lead to O. Therefore, any path from H that goes through G or F *must* eventually pass through node E to reach the part of the graph connected to O.
        *   Examples: H-G-F-E-I-C-O, H-F-E-M-J-P-O.
    *   **Paths starting with H-B:**
        *   H is connected to B. Any path starting this way must pass through B.
        *   Examples: H-B-C-O, H-B-I-J-P-O.
    *   **Paths starting with H-I:**
        *   H is connected to I. Any path starting this way must pass through I.
        *   Examples: H-I-C-O, H-I-P-O. (Note: Paths like H-I-E-... would also be covered by the first group if E is removed).

3.  **Identify Potential Cut Nodes:** To disconnect H from O, I need to ensure that all these categories of paths are broken.
    *   To break all paths in the "H-G/F" category, removing node **E** is necessary and sufficient, as E is the only bridge from the G/F cluster to the rest of the graph leading to O.
    *   To break all paths in the "H-B" category, removing node **B** is necessary and sufficient.
    *   To break all paths in the "H-I" category, removing node **I** is necessary and sufficient.

4.  **Formulate a Cut Set:** Based on the above analysis, the set of nodes {E, B, I} would disconnect H from O.
    *   If E is removed, all paths H-G-F-E-... and H-F-E-... are broken.
    *   If B is removed, all paths H-B-... are broken.
    *   If I is removed, all paths H-I-... are broken.
    *   Since these three nodes cover all initial branches from H that lead to O, their removal ensures no path remains.

5.  **Verify Minimality:** I need to check if a smaller set of nodes (i.e., 1 or 2 nodes) can also disconnect H and O.
    *   **Single Node Removal:** As shown in the step-by-step analysis, removing any single node (e.g., E, B, I, C, P, J, M, L, G, F, K, X, N, Y, D) leaves at least one path between H and O. For example, removing E leaves paths like H-B-C-O and H-I-C-O. Removing B leaves paths like H-I-C-O and H-G-F-E-I-C-O. Removing I leaves paths like H-B-C-O and H-G-F-E-M-J-P-O.
    *   **Two Node Removal:**
        *   If I remove {E, B}: Paths through I (e.g., H-I-C-O) still exist.
        *   If I remove {E, I}: Paths through B (e.g., H-B-C-O) still exist.
        *   If I remove {B, I}: Paths through E (e.g., H-G-F-E-M-J-P-O) still exist.
    *   Therefore, no set of 1 or 2 nodes can disconnect H and O.

6.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 3. The cut set {E, B, I} achieves this disconnection. Another valid cut set of size 3 is {C, P, E}.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [E, B, I]

3.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Edge-Disjoint Paths):**
    To confirm if 3 is indeed the minimum, I will try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and J. If I can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

    Let's find edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - F - K - J
        Edges: {(E,F), (F,K), (K,J)}

    *   **Path 2:** E - B - C - I - J
        Edges: {(E,B), (B,C), (C,I), (I,J)}
        Let's check for disjointness with Path 1: All edges in Path 2 are distinct from Path 1.

    *   **Path 3:** E - M - L - B - N - O - P - J
        Edges: {(E,M), (M,L), (L,B), (B,N), (N,O), (O,P), (P,J)}
        Let's check for disjointness with Path 1 and Path 2:
        *   (E,M) is not in Path 1 or Path 2.
        *   (M,L) is not in Path 1 or Path 2.
        *   (L,B) is not in Path 1 or Path 2.
        *   (B,N) is not in Path 1 or Path 2.
        *   (N,O) is not in Path 1 or Path 2.
        *   (O,P) is not in Path 1 or Path 2.
        *   (P,J) is not in Path 1 or Path 2.
        All edges in Path 3 are distinct from Path 1 and Path 2.

    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

4.  **Confirm the minimum cut:**
    As established in step 2, the cut set [(J, K), (J, I), (J, P)] consists of 3 edges.
    *   Removing (J, K) breaks all paths that go through K to J (e.g., E-F-K-J).
    *   Removing (J, I) breaks all paths that go through I to J (e.g., E-B-C-I-J).
    *   Removing (J, P) breaks all paths that go through P to J (e.g., E-M-L-B-N-O-P-J).
    *   Node D is connected to J and P. With (J,P) removed, D cannot provide an alternative path to J from E.
    Therefore, removing these 3 edges completely disconnects J from E.

Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths (proving the minimum is at least 3) and identified a cut set of 3 edges that achieves disconnection, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    *   Neighbors of H: B, G, I
3.  **Analyze paths from M:** All paths from M to H must start by traversing an edge from M to one of its direct neighbors: L, E, or J.
    *   **Paths starting M -> L:** The only connection from L (other than M) is to E. So, any path from M through L must go M -> L -> E -> ...
    *   **Paths starting M -> E:** These paths go M -> E -> ...
    *   **Paths starting M -> J:** These paths go M -> J -> ...
4.  **Consider potential cut sets:**
    *   **Can a single node disconnect M and H?**
        *   Removing L: M can still reach H via E or J.
        *   Removing E: M can still reach H via J.
        *   Removing J: M can still reach H via L or E.
        *   Removing B, G, or I (neighbors of H): M can still reach H via the other neighbors.
        *   Removing any other single node (e.g., F, K, X, D, P, O, Y, N, C): There would always be alternative paths.
        *   Therefore, a cut set of size 1 is not possible.
    *   **Consider a cut set of size 2:**
        *   Let's examine the "entry points" from M to the rest of the graph. These are L, E, and J.
        *   If we remove node E:
            *   All paths starting M -> E -> ... are broken.
            *   Additionally, node L is only connected to M and E. If E is removed, L becomes isolated with M. Therefore, all paths starting M -> L -> E -> ... are also broken.
            *   After removing E, the only remaining way for M to reach H is through J (M -> J -> ...).
        *   If we then remove node J:
            *   All paths starting M -> J -> ... are broken.
        *   With both E and J removed, M is connected only to L, and L is connected only to M (since E is removed). This means M and L form a disconnected component, completely isolated from the rest of the graph, including H.
        *   Therefore, removing nodes E and J will completely disconnect M from H.

5.  **Verify the cut set {E, J}:**
    *   All paths from M must begin with M-L, M-E, or M-J.
    *   Removing E breaks all M-E paths. It also isolates L with M, effectively breaking all M-L-E paths.
    *   Removing J breaks all M-J paths.
    *   Since all possible initial connections from M are severed, no path can exist from M to H.
    *   This cut set has a size of 2, which is the minimum possible since a size 1 cut set was ruled out.

The final answer is as follows:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, J]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': ['J', 'E']}
Progress: 280/280 images processed.
